<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the metric Blaschke conjecture: every connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold of positive dimension whose global injectivity radius equals its diameter is, up to scale, a standard compact rank-one symmetric space.

- **claim-metric-blaschke** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-blaschke)): Let $(M,g)$ be a connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold of positive dimension. If $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$, then $(M,g)$ is isometric, up to multiplication of the metric by a positive constant, to a round sphere, a standard real, complex or quaternionic projective space, or the Cayley plane.
  - Explanation: Every connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold of positive dimension whose injectivity radius equals its diameter is, after a constant rescaling of the metric, a round sphere, a standard real, complex or quaternionic projective space, or the Cayley plane.
- **claim-sharp-volume-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-comparison)): Every Blaschke manifold and its model at the same diameter satisfy $$\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M,g)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M_0,g_0).$$ Equality holds if and only if $(M,g)$ is isometric to $(M_0,g_0)$.
  - Explanation: The volume of any Blaschke manifold is at least that of its standard model scaled to the same diameter, and equality happens only for an isometry with the model.
- **claim-volume-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-volume-reduction)): If a Blaschke manifold has volume at most that of its model at the same diameter, then it is isometric to that model.
  - Explanation: Together with the sharp comparison, an upper volume bound by the model volume at equal diameter already forces isometry with the model, so rigidity reduces to proving an upper bound.
- **claim-cut-time** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-cut-time)): Let $D=\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$. Every unit-speed geodesic segment of length at most $D$ is minimizing. A segment of length less than $D$ is the unique minimizing segment between its endpoints, and no segment of length greater than $D$ is minimizing.
  - Explanation: Equality of injectivity radius and diameter makes geodesics minimize exactly up to the common length, uniquely before it and never beyond it.
- **claim-classical-structure** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-structure)): Every unit-speed geodesic is simply closed with period $2D$. For each $p\in M$, the map $v\mapsto\exp_p(Dv)$ from $S_pM$ onto the cut locus is a smooth sphere fibration whose fibers are great spheres of one dimension $k$, independent of $p$. Its differential has nullity $k$. The model is determined as follows:* 1. *If $k=n-1$, the model is a sphere; if $k=0$, it is a real projective space. In these two cases the metric classification is known: the manifold is isometric to its equally scaled model.* 2. *If $0<k<n-1$, the manifold is simply connected and its integral cohomology ring has one generator of degree $k+1$. The possibilities are complex-projective type, quaternionic-projective type, and Cayley-plane type, with the dimensions listed below.
  - Explanation: Classical Blaschke structure: all geodesics are simply closed of the same period, the cut locus is the base of a sphere fibration by great spheres of constant dimension, and this determines the model type.
- **claim-jacobi-determinant** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lag-determinant)): Choose $G_x$- and $G_y$-orthonormal frames $Z_x=(Z_{x,1},\ldots,Z_{x,d})$ and $Z_y=(Z_{y,1},\ldots,Z_{y,d})$ of $L_x$ and $L_y$. Then $$f(x,y)=\left\|\det\bigl(\omega(Z_{x,a},Z_{y,b})\bigr)_{a,b=1}^d\right\| \label{eq:lag-f}$$ is independent of these choices. It is continuous, symmetric in its arguments, and separately $T$-periodic. It is the absolute normal Jacobi determinant at $y$ of fields vanishing at $x$ with orthonormal initial derivatives there. In particular, for $0<y-x<D$ it is the polar volume density with respect to $dt$ and ordinary sphere area at $\gamma(x)$.* *Use the same orthonormal basis of $\dot\gamma(0)^\perp$ for the coordinates $$(q,p)=(J(0),J'(0))\in\mathbb R^d\oplus\mathbb R^d,$$ choosing its first $k$ vectors to be the initial derivatives of a basis of $L_0\cap L_D$. On $$\mathcal I=(0,D)\cup(D,T)$$ there is a smooth symmetric matrix $A(t)$ such that $L_t=\{(q,A(t)q):q\in\mathbb R^d\}$, and $A'(t)>0$. For $x,y\in\mathcal I$, $$f(x,y)= \frac{\|\det(A(y)-A(x))\|} {\sqrt{\det A'(x)\det A'(y)}}. \label{eq:lag-graph-determinant}$$
  - Explanation: The absolute Jacobi determinant of a closed geodesic is well defined by orthonormal frames of the vanishing-field Lagrangians, is continuous, symmetric and periodic, and in a fixed graph chart it has an explicit quotient formula in the matrix and its derivative.
- **claim-pole-expansions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lag-poles)): There is a positive definite $k\times k$ matrix $B$ such that the graph matrix has the expansions $$\begin{aligned} A(t)&=-\frac1{t-D} \begin{pmatrix}B&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}+R_D(t), &&\text{near }D,\label{eq:lag-middle-pole}\\ A(t)&=-\frac1{t-t_0}\mathrm{Id}_d+R_{t_0}(t), &&\text{near }t_0=0,T,\label{eq:lag-end-poles} \end{aligned}$$ where each remainder extends smoothly across its indicated point. In particular, $C$ and $H$ extend smoothly across $D$, and $H'(D)>0$.
  - Explanation: Near the cut time the graph matrix has a simple pole confined to the first block with a positive definite residue, near the endpoints it has the identity residue, and the complementary block stays smooth with positive derivative.
- **claim-scalar-profiles** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lag-profiles)): Define $$\begin{aligned} h(t)&=\det(H'(t))^{1/m} &&(0<t<T),\\ s(t)&=\det(S(t))^{1/k} &&(t\in\mathcal I),\\ s_i(t)&=s(t+iD) &&(0<t<D,\ i=0,1). \end{aligned} \label{eq:lag-profiles}$$ The function $h$ is smooth and positive across $D$. There are positive constants $c_H,C_H,c_i,C_i$, depending on the fixed geodesic and its chart, such that $$\begin{aligned} c_H&\le h(t)\sin^2t\le C_H &&(0<t<T), \label{eq:lag-h-bounds}\\ c_i&\le \frac{s_i(t)\sin^2(2t)}4\le C_i &&(0<t<D,\ i=0,1). \label{eq:lag-s-bounds} \end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the logarithms of the ratios of $h$ to $1/\sin^2t$ and of $s_i$ to $4/\sin^2(2t)$ are bounded.
  - Explanation: The determinant roots of the two matrix blocks are comparable, up to constants depending on the geodesic and chart, to the model reciprocal-square-sine profiles, so their logarithmic ratios are bounded.
- **claim-schur-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lag-schur)): The matrix $Q(x,y)$ is symmetric and satisfies $$\det(\Delta A)=\det(\Delta H)\det Q(x,y),\qquad Q(y,x)=-Q(x,y). \label{eq:lag-Schur-factorization}$$ It extends continuously by zero to $y=x\in\mathcal I$, and $$Q(u,u+D)=Q(u+D,u)=0\qquad(0<u<D). \label{eq:lag-Q-zeros}$$ Whenever its distinct endpoints remain in $\mathcal I$, it obeys the positive-semidefinite matrix inequalities $$\partial_yQ(x,y)\ge S(y),\qquad -\partial_xQ(x,y)\ge S(x). \label{eq:lag-Q-derivatives}$$ Consequently, for every $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\in\{0,1\}$, $$\begin{aligned} Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\ge\int_u^v S(t+jD)\,dt, \label{eq:lag-Q-bound-j}\\ Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\ge\int_u^v S(t+iD)\,dt. \label{eq:lag-Q-bound-i} \end{aligned}$$ In particular, this $Q$ is positive definite for all four choices of $i,j$, including $i=1,j=0$.
  - Explanation: The Schur complement of the regular block in the endpoint difference is symmetric, antisymmetric under exchange of endpoints, vanishes at cut pairs and has derivatives bounded below by the velocity Schur complement, for either endpoint order.
- **claim-scalar-block-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lag-block-bounds)): With the profiles of Proposition <a href="#lag:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:profiles">8</a>, every pair of distinct chart endpoints satisfies $$f_H(x,y)^{1/m}\ge \frac{\left\|\int_x^y h(t)\,dt\right\|}{\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}}. \label{eq:lag-H-scalar-bound}$$ For $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\in\{0,1\}$ one has $$f_K(u+iD,v+jD)^{1/k}\ge \frac{ \left(\int_u^v s_i(t)\,dt\right)^{1/2} \left(\int_u^v s_j(t)\,dt\right)^{1/2}} {\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)}}. \label{eq:lag-K-scalar-bound}$$ Together with endpoint exchange, these bounds cover the full square $(0,T)^2$ except the chart, diagonal, and cut lines, a set of planar measure zero.
  - Explanation: Both determinant factors are bounded below by scalar quotients of interval integrals of the profiles, using the determinant-root concavity inequality, and these bounds cover almost the whole square of endpoint pairs.
- **claim-weighted-scalar-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-weighted-scalar)): Let $b>0$, $\lambda=\pi/b$, and $$p_0(t)=\frac{\lambda^2}{\sin^2(\lambda t)},\qquad 0<t<b.$$ Let $p:(0,b)\to(0,\infty)$ be continuous and suppose that $g=\log(p/p_0)$ is bounded. Let $W$ be nonnegative and measurable on $(0,b)$, with $W(b-z)=W(z)$ almost everywhere and $W(z)/\sin(\lambda z)$ essentially bounded. Write $$\Delta_b=\{(x,y):0<x<y<b\},\qquad d\nu_{xy}(t)=\frac{p_0(t)\,dt}{\int_x^y p_0},\quad x<t<y.$$ Then all the following integrals are finite, and $$\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x) \left[\log\frac{\int_x^y p}{\int_x^y p_0} -\frac{g(x)+g(y)}2\right]dx\,dy \notag\\ &\qquad=\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x) \left[\log\int_x^y e^{g(t)}\,d\nu_{xy}(t) -\int_x^y g(t)\,d\nu_{xy}(t)\right]dx\,dy \ \geq\ 0.\label{weighted:scalar-identity} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: A logarithmic form of Kazdan's weighted cancellation: for a reflection-symmetric weight, the integrated endpoint terms equal the integrated interval averages, and Jensen's inequality makes the common integral nonnegative.
- **claim-geodesic-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-weighted-geodesic)): In the normalized intermediate case of Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a>, let $f(x,y)$ be the Jacobi determinant of any fixed unit-speed geodesic, as in Proposition <a href="#lag:determinant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:determinant">6</a>. Put $$\mathcal R=(0,T)^2,\qquad w(z)=(\sin z)^m\left\|\frac{\sin(2z)}2\right\|^k \quad(0\leq z\leq T).$$ Then $$\int_{\mathcal R}f(x,y)\,dx\,dy \ \geq\ \int_{\mathcal R}w(\|y-x\|)\,dx\,dy. \label{weighted:comparison}$$ Equality holds if and only if $f(x,y)=w(\|y-x\|)$ for every $(x,y)\in[0,T]^2$.
  - Explanation: Along any closed geodesic the integral of the Jacobi determinant over the square of times is at least the integral of the model density, with equality exactly when the determinant equals the model density pointwise.
- **claim-polar-integration** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-polar)): For every $p\in M$, $$\int_{S_pM}\int_0^D J(\xi,t)\,dt\,d\sigma_p(\xi)=V, \qquad \int_{S_pM}\int_0^T J(\xi,t)\,dt\,d\sigma_p(\xi)=2V.$$
  - Explanation: Integrating the polar density over the unit sphere at any point and over half a period gives the volume of the manifold, and over a full period gives twice that volume.
- **claim-cut-incidence-normal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-inc-normal)): The cut incidence $$F=\{(p,q)\in M\times M:q\in C_p\}$$ is a smooth embedded submanifold. Both projections are smooth fiber bundles with fiber a cut locus. If $u_1,u_2$ are the two pullbacks of $u$, its rank-four normal bundle $\mathcal V$ can be oriented so that $$\label{inc:normal-classes} e(\mathcal V)=u_1-u_2,\qquad p_1(\mathcal V)=2s(u_1+u_2),\qquad s\in2\mathbb Z+1.$$ Projection of $\mathcal V_{(p,q)}$ into $T_pM$ identifies it with $N_pC_q$. The unit sphere of this four-plane is exactly the great sphere of initial directions of minimizing geodesics from $p$ to $q$.
  - Explanation: Cut pairs form a smooth embedded submanifold with both projections fiber bundles, and its actual normal four-plane bundle has Euler class and first Pontryagin class given by the stated formulas with an odd integer coefficient, and its unit sphere is the great sphere of minimizing directions.
- **claim-total-pontryagin** (theorem, [source](#stmt-inc-pontryagin)): The total Pontryagin class of $M$ is $$\label{inc:pontryagin-formula} p(TM)=\frac{(1+2su+u^2)^{r+1}}{1+4su} \quad\text{in }H^*(M;\mathbb Q).$$ The quotient means its power-series expansion truncated by $u^{r+1}=0$.
  - Explanation: The rank constraint from two disjoint direction planes determines the total Pontryagin class of a quaternionic-type Blaschke manifold in terms of the odd coefficient.
- **claim-signature-coefficient** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sig-coefficient)): In Proposition <a href="#inc:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:normal">14</a>, $\|s\|=1$. In particular, the actual tautological four-plane bundle of every tangent great-sphere fibration at a point of $M$ satisfies $p_1=\pm2e$.
  - Explanation: The signature theorem, with exact coefficients in small ranks and a contour-integral estimate in larger ranks, forces the incidence coefficient to have absolute value one, so the tautological four-plane bundle has first Pontryagin class equal to plus or minus twice its Euler class.
- **claim-contact-volume** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-contact)): With this orientation and Euler class, $$\label{geo:contact-formula} N:=\int_B e^{4r-1} =\frac{(4r-1)!\,\|S^{4r-1}\|\,\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M)}{T^{4r}}>0.$$ For the standard quaternionic model at the same diameter, this number is $$\label{geo:model-number} N_0=\frac{1}{2r+1}\binom{4r}{2r}.$$
  - Explanation: The top power of the Euler class on the space of oriented geodesics equals an explicit positive multiple of the manifold volume, and for the standard quaternionic model it equals a central binomial coefficient divided by an odd integer.
- **claim-integral-gysin** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-gysin)): The manifolds $U$ and $B$ are simply connected. There is an integral class $v\in H^4(B;\mathbb Z)$ with $\rho^*v=z$. For $2j<n$, the following is an integral basis: $$\label{geo:low-basis} H^{2j}(B;\mathbb Z):\qquad e^{j-2a}v^a,\qquad 0\le a\le\lfloor j/2\rfloor.$$ All odd cohomology of $B$ vanishes, and its even cohomology is free abelian. In particular $H^2(B;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Ze$ and $H^4(B;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Ze^2\oplus\mathbb Zv$. The rational cohomology ring is generated by $e,v$, and $$\label{geo:total-betti} \dim_{\mathbb Q}H^*(B;\mathbb Q)=2r(r+1).$$* *Multiplication by $e$ at the middle fits into an exact sequence $$\label{geo:middle-sequence} 0\longrightarrow H^{n-2}(B;\mathbb Z) \xrightarrow{\ e\smile\ }H^n(B;\mathbb Z) \longrightarrow\mathbb Z/(r+1)\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently the integral bilinear form $$\label{geo:middle-form} (a,b)\longmapsto\int_B eab,\qquad a,b\in H^{n-2}(B;\mathbb Z),$$ has determinant of absolute value $r+1$ in any integral basis. The map $e\smile:H^n(B;\mathbb Z)\to H^{n+2}(B;\mathbb Z)$ is an isomorphism.
  - Explanation: The unit tangent bundle and the space of geodesics are simply connected, the low-degree integral cohomology of the latter has an explicit basis, and the middle pairing has determinant of absolute value one more than the quaternionic rank.
- **claim-first-attachment** (construction, [source](#stmt-geo-attachment)): There is an oriented rank-three vector bundle $E\to B$ and an attaching map $$f:S(E)\longrightarrow M.$$ Let $p:S(E)\to B$ and put $x=f^*u\in H^4(S(E);\mathbb Z)$. The following properties hold:* 1. *The pullback $\rho^*E$ is the first negative bundle for the free-loop energy. It is isomorphic as a real vector bundle to the rank-three bundle $K\to U$ with fiber $$K_w=\{a\in w^\perp: J_a(D)=0\}, \quad J_a(0)=0,\quad J_a'(0)=a,$$ where $J_a$ is the normal Jacobi field along $\gamma_w$.* 2. *Let $\widetilde p:S(\rho^*E)\to U$ and $\widehat\rho:S(\rho^*E)\to S(E)$ be the pullback square. Then $$\label{geo:attachment-pullback} \widehat\rho^*x=\widetilde p^*z.$$* 3. *After choosing the orientation of $E$, one has $$\label{geo:primitive-boundary} p_!x=e.$$ With either orientation its coefficient is $\pm1$.* 4. *The actual negative bundle satisfies $$\label{geo:negative-pontryagin} \rho^*p_1(E)=4z.$$
  - Explanation: The first negative bundle of the free-loop energy descends to a rank-three bundle on the geodesic space, whose sphere bundle attaches to the manifold by a map pulling back the generator in a way that gives a unit fiber integral and a first Pontryagin class equal to four times the generator.
- **claim-arithmetic-parameters** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-parameters)): Let $p:S(E)\to B$ and $x$ be as in Proposition <a href="#geo:attachment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:attachment">19</a>, and let $$\xi=\frac12e(T_{\mathrm{vert}}S(E))\in H^2(S(E);\mathbb Q), \qquad X=e^2\in H^4(B;\mathbb Z).$$ There is a class $Y\in H^4(B;\mathbb Q)$ such that, with pullbacks by $p$ suppressed, $$\label{geo:sphere-parameters} x=Y+e\xi,\qquad \xi^2=p_1(E)/4,\qquad p_!\xi=1,\qquad \rho^*Y=z,\qquad 2Y\in H^4(B;\mathbb Z).$$ For an integer $b$, $$\label{geo:Y-lift} Y=v+\frac b2e^2.$$ There are rational numbers $\alpha,\beta$ with $$\label{geo:L-parameters} L:=e^2p_1(E)/4=\alpha X^2+\beta XY,\qquad 4\alpha\in\mathbb Z,\qquad \|\beta\|=1.$$ Here $\beta=1$ for <a href="#geo:incidence-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:incidence-input">[geo:incidence-input]</a>; retaining $\beta$ also allows the opposite generator convention, for which $\beta=-1$. Moreover $$\label{geo:two-nilpotents} (Y+e\xi)^{r+1}=(Y-e\xi)^{r+1}=0 \quad\text{in }H^*(S(E);\mathbb Q).$$* *Count $X,Y$ as polynomial degree one, corresponding to cohomological degree four. For homogeneous polynomials of degree $2r-1$, set $$\ell(P)=\int_B eP(X,Y).$$ Then the middle moment matrix satisfies $$\label{geo:moment-determinant} \left\|\det\left[ \ell\big(X^{2r-1-i-j}Y^{i+j}\big) \right]_{0\le i,j<r}\right\|=r+1, \qquad \ell(X^{2r-1})=N.$$ Pullback $p^*:H^*(B;\mathbb Q)\to H^*(S(E);\mathbb Q)$ is injective, and multiplication by $e$ on $H^n(B;\mathbb Q)$ is injective.
  - Explanation: The attachment class splits into a rational base class and a vertical part, the base class is half-integral and a shift of the integral lift, the Pontryagin-type class has controlled rational parameters, both signed powers of the attachment class vanish, and the middle moment determinant has absolute value one more than the rank.
- **claim-power-relations** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-relations)): The classes $Q(X,Y)$ and $R(X,Y)$ vanish in $H^*(B;\mathbb Q)$. Moreover $\alpha\ne0$, and consequently $\|4\alpha\|\ge1$.
  - Explanation: The two polynomial relations coming from nilpotence of the attachment class vanish in the rational cohomology of the geodesic space, and the quadratic parameter is nonzero with a quantitative lower bound.
- **claim-complete-intersection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-complete-intersection)): For $\alpha\ne0$, the polynomials $Q,R$ have no common nonzero zero over $\mathbb C$. The algebra $$\mathcal A=\mathbb C[X,Y]/(Q,R)$$ has Hilbert series $$\frac{(1-t^r)(1-t^{r+1})}{(1-t)^2} =(1+t+\cdots+t^{r-1})(1+t+\cdots+t^r). \label{alg:hilbert}$$ In particular its top degree is $2r-1$, with dimension one, and $$X:\mathcal A_{r-1}\longrightarrow\mathcal A_r \quad\hbox{is an isomorphism},\qquad \dim\mathcal A_{r-1}=\dim\mathcal A_r=r. \label{alg:middle-isomorphism}$$ Furthermore the complete rational relation ideal of the classes $e,Y$ is generated by $Q(e^2,Y)$ and $R(e^2,Y)$.
  - Explanation: For a nonzero parameter the two relation polynomials are coprime, the quotient algebra has the Hilbert series of a complete intersection with one-dimensional top degree, multiplication by the square of the Euler class is an isomorphism in the middle, and the relations generate the full rational relation ideal.
- **claim-top-functional-determinant** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-determinant)): The functional $\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}$ descends to the top degree of $\mathcal A$ and is nonzero. If $$J_j=\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}(X^{2r-1-j}Y^j),$$ then for every $\alpha\ne0$ and every $\beta\in\mathbb C$, $$\det(J_{i+j})_{0\le i,j<r} =(-1)^{r(r+1)/2}(r+1)(4\alpha)^{-r(r-1)/2}. \label{alg:determinant-formula}$$ For the geometric parameters there is consequently a scalar $K$ with $$\ell=K\Phi_{\alpha,\beta},\qquad \|K\|=\|4\alpha\|^{(r-1)/2}. \label{alg:normalization}$$
  - Explanation: An explicit coefficient-extraction functional descends to the top degree of the quotient algebra, is nonzero, has a moment determinant independent of the second parameter, and so normalizes the geometric top functional up to a scalar of controlled size.
- **claim-quaternionic-upper-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-upper)): For a quaternionic-type Blaschke manifold of dimension $4r$, $r\ge2$, the contact characteristic number of Proposition <a href="#geo:contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:contact">17</a> satisfies $$N=\int_{B}e^{4r-1} \le \frac1{2r+1}\binom{4r}{2r}. \label{alg:sharp-upper}$$
  - Explanation: The contact characteristic number of a quaternionic-type Blaschke manifold is at most the model value, obtained by bounding each summand of the explicit coefficient sum and evaluating the extremal formal parameters.
- **claim-complex-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-complex)): A Blaschke manifold of complex-projective type is isometric to its equally scaled standard complex projective model.
  - Explanation: A Blaschke manifold of complex-projective type has the homotopy type of the model, so Yang's volume theorem and the volume reduction give isometry with the equally scaled standard model.
- **claim-planes-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-planes)): A Blaschke manifold with model $\mathbb H\mathrm P^2$ or $\mathbb O\mathrm P^2$ is isometric to that model at the same diameter.
  - Explanation: For the quaternionic and Cayley planes, the classification of projective-plane-like manifolds and the equal-volume theorem for manifolds with one common least geodesic period give the model volume, hence isometry.
- **claim-quaternionic-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-quaternionic)): A Blaschke manifold of quaternionic type is isometric to its standard quaternionic model at the same diameter.
  - Explanation: The sharp upper bound on the contact number translates through the contact formula into a volume upper bound by the model, so the volume reduction gives isometry with the standard quaternionic model.

In these two cases the metric classification is known: the manifold is isometric to its equally scaled model.*\n\n2.  *If $0<k<n-1$, the manifold is simply connected and its integral cohomology ring has one generator of degree $k+1$. The possibilities are complex-projective type, quaternionic-projective type, and Cayley-plane type, with the dimensions listed below.",
          "source_id": "PM-344.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lag-determinant",
        "exact_quote": "Choose $G_x$- and $G_y$-orthonormal frames $Z_x=(Z_{x,1},\\ldots,Z_{x,d})$ and $Z_y=(Z_{y,1},\\ldots,Z_{y,d})$ of $L_x$ and $L_y$. Then $$f(x,y)=\\left|\\det\\bigl(\\omega(Z_{x,a},Z_{y,b})\\bigr)_{a,b=1}^d\\right|\n \\label{eq:lag-f}$$ is independent of these choices. It is continuous, symmetric in its arguments, and separately $T$-periodic. It is the absolute normal Jacobi determinant at $y$ of fields vanishing at $x$ with orthonormal initial derivatives there. In particular, for $0<y-x<D$ it is the polar volume density with respect to $dt$ and ordinary sphere area at $\\gamma(x)$.*\n\n\n*Use the same orthonormal basis of $\\dot\\gamma(0)^\\perp$ for the coordinates $$(q,p)=(J(0),J'(0))\\in\\mathbb R^d\\oplus\\mathbb R^d,$$ choosing its first $k$ vectors to be the initial derivatives of a basis of $L_0\\cap L_D$. On $$\\mathcal I=(0,D)\\cup(D,T)$$ there is a smooth symmetric matrix $A(t)$ such that $L_t=\\{(q,A(t)q):q\\in\\mathbb R^d\\}$, and $A'(t)>0$. For $x,y\\in\\mathcal I$, $$f(x,y)=\n \\frac{|\\det(A(y)-A(x))|}\n {\\sqrt{\\det A'(x)\\det A'(y)}}.\n \\label{eq:lag-graph-determinant}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lag-profiles",
        "exact_quote": "Define $$\\begin{aligned}\n h(t)&=\\det(H'(t))^{1/m} &&(0<t<T),\\\\\n s(t)&=\\det(S(t))^{1/k} &&(t\\in\\mathcal I),\\\\\n s_i(t)&=s(t+iD) &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\end{aligned}\n \\label{eq:lag-profiles}$$ The function $h$ is smooth and positive across $D$. There are positive constants $c_H,C_H,c_i,C_i$, depending on the fixed geodesic and its chart, such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n c_H&\\le h(t)\\sin^2t\\le C_H &&(0<t<T),\n \\label{eq:lag-h-bounds}\\\\\n c_i&\\le \\frac{s_i(t)\\sin^2(2t)}4\\le C_i\n &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\label{eq:lag-s-bounds}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the logarithms of the ratios of $h$ to $1/\\sin^2t$ and of $s_i$ to $4/\\sin^2(2t)$ are bounded.",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lag-profiles",
          "exact_quote": "Define $$\\begin{aligned}\n h(t)&=\\det(H'(t))^{1/m} &&(0<t<T),\\\\\n s(t)&=\\det(S(t))^{1/k} &&(t\\in\\mathcal I),\\\\\n s_i(t)&=s(t+iD) &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\end{aligned}\n \\label{eq:lag-profiles}$$ The function $h$ is smooth and positive across $D$. There are positive constants $c_H,C_H,c_i,C_i$, depending on the fixed geodesic and its chart, such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n c_H&\\le h(t)\\sin^2t\\le C_H &&(0<t<T),\n \\label{eq:lag-h-bounds}\\\\\n c_i&\\le \\frac{s_i(t)\\sin^2(2t)}4\\le C_i\n &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\label{eq:lag-s-bounds}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the logarithms of the ratios of $h$ to $1/\\sin^2t$ and of $s_i$ to $4/\\sin^2(2t)$ are bounded.",

      "exact_statement": "Define $$\\begin{aligned}\n h(t)&=\\det(H'(t))^{1/m} &&(0<t<T),\\\\\n s(t)&=\\det(S(t))^{1/k} &&(t\\in\\mathcal I),\\\\\n s_i(t)&=s(t+iD) &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\end{aligned}\n \\label{eq:lag-profiles}$$ The function $h$ is smooth and positive across $D$. There are positive constants $c_H,C_H,c_i,C_i$, depending on the fixed geodesic and its chart, such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n c_H&\\le h(t)\\sin^2t\\le C_H &&(0<t<T),\n \\label{eq:lag-h-bounds}\\\\\n c_i&\\le \\frac{s_i(t)\\sin^2(2t)}4\\le C_i\n &&(0<t<D,\\ i=0,1).\n \\label{eq:lag-s-bounds}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the logarithms of the ratios of $h$ to $1/\\sin^2t$ and of $s_i$ to $4/\\sin^2(2t)$ are bounded.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lag-schur",
        "exact_quote": "The matrix $Q(x,y)$ is symmetric and satisfies $$\\det(\\Delta A)=\\det(\\Delta H)\\det Q(x,y),\\qquad\n Q(y,x)=-Q(x,y).\n \\label{eq:lag-Schur-factorization}$$ It extends continuously by zero to $y=x\\in\\mathcal I$, and $$Q(u,u+D)=Q(u+D,u)=0\\qquad(0<u<D).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-zeros}$$ Whenever its distinct endpoints remain in $\\mathcal I$, it obeys the positive-semidefinite matrix inequalities $$\\partial_yQ(x,y)\\ge S(y),\\qquad\n -\\partial_xQ(x,y)\\ge S(x).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-derivatives}$$ Consequently, for every $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+jD)\\,dt,\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-j}\\\\\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+iD)\\,dt.\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-i}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular, this $Q$ is positive definite for all four choices of $i,j$, including $i=1,j=0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lag-schur",
          "exact_quote": "The matrix $Q(x,y)$ is symmetric and satisfies $$\\det(\\Delta A)=\\det(\\Delta H)\\det Q(x,y),\\qquad\n Q(y,x)=-Q(x,y).\n \\label{eq:lag-Schur-factorization}$$ It extends continuously by zero to $y=x\\in\\mathcal I$, and $$Q(u,u+D)=Q(u+D,u)=0\\qquad(0<u<D).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-zeros}$$ Whenever its distinct endpoints remain in $\\mathcal I$, it obeys the positive-semidefinite matrix inequalities $$\\partial_yQ(x,y)\\ge S(y),\\qquad\n -\\partial_xQ(x,y)\\ge S(x).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-derivatives}$$ Consequently, for every $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+jD)\\,dt,\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-j}\\\\\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+iD)\\,dt.\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-i}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular, this $Q$ is positive definite for all four choices of $i,j$, including $i=1,j=0$.",
          "source_id": "PM-344.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "The matrix $Q(x,y)$ is symmetric and satisfies $$\\det(\\Delta A)=\\det(\\Delta H)\\det Q(x,y),\\qquad\n Q(y,x)=-Q(x,y).\n \\label{eq:lag-Schur-factorization}$$ It extends continuously by zero to $y=x\\in\\mathcal I$, and $$Q(u,u+D)=Q(u+D,u)=0\\qquad(0<u<D).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-zeros}$$ Whenever its distinct endpoints remain in $\\mathcal I$, it obeys the positive-semidefinite matrix inequalities $$\\partial_yQ(x,y)\\ge S(y),\\qquad\n -\\partial_xQ(x,y)\\ge S(x).\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-derivatives}$$ Consequently, for every $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+jD)\\,dt,\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-j}\\\\\n Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\\ge\\int_u^v S(t+iD)\\,dt.\n \\label{eq:lag-Q-bound-i}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular, this $Q$ is positive definite for all four choices of $i,j$, including $i=1,j=0$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lag-block-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "With the profiles of Proposition <a href=\"#lag:profiles\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lag:profiles\">8</a>, every pair of distinct chart endpoints satisfies $$f_H(x,y)^{1/m}\\ge\n \\frac{\\left|\\int_x^y h(t)\\,dt\\right|}{\\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}}.\n \\label{eq:lag-H-scalar-bound}$$ For $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}$ one has $$f_K(u+iD,v+jD)^{1/k}\\ge\n \\frac{\n  \\left(\\int_u^v s_i(t)\\,dt\\right)^{1/2}\n  \\left(\\int_u^v s_j(t)\\,dt\\right)^{1/2}}\n {\\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)}}.\n \\label{eq:lag-K-scalar-bound}$$ Together with endpoint exchange, these bounds cover the full square $(0,T)^2$ except the chart, diagonal, and cut lines, a set of planar measure zero.",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-344.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "With the profiles of Proposition <a href=\"#lag:profiles\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lag:profiles\">8</a>, every pair of distinct chart endpoints satisfies $$f_H(x,y)^{1/m}\\ge\n \\frac{\\left|\\int_x^y h(t)\\,dt\\right|}{\\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}}.\n \\label{eq:lag-H-scalar-bound}$$ For $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}$ one has $$f_K(u+iD,v+jD)^{1/k}\\ge\n \\frac{\n  \\left(\\int_u^v s_i(t)\\,dt\\right)^{1/2}\n  \\left(\\int_u^v s_j(t)\\,dt\\right)^{1/2}}\n {\\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)}}.\n \\label{eq:lag-K-scalar-bound}$$ Together with endpoint exchange, these bounds cover the full square $(0,T)^2$ except the chart, diagonal, and cut lines, a set of planar measure zero.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-weighted-scalar",
        "exact_quote": "Let $b>0$, $\\lambda=\\pi/b$, and $$p_0(t)=\\frac{\\lambda^2}{\\sin^2(\\lambda t)},\\qquad 0<t<b.$$ Let $p:(0,b)\\to(0,\\infty)$ be continuous and suppose that $g=\\log(p/p_0)$ is bounded. Let $W$ be nonnegative and measurable on $(0,b)$, with $W(b-z)=W(z)$ almost everywhere and $W(z)/\\sin(\\lambda z)$ essentially bounded. Write $$\\Delta_b=\\{(x,y):0<x<y<b\\},\\qquad\n d\\nu_{xy}(t)=\\frac{p_0(t)\\,dt}{\\int_x^y p_0},\\quad x<t<y.$$ Then all the following integrals are finite, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\frac{\\int_x^y p}{\\int_x^y p_0}\n       -\\frac{g(x)+g(y)}2\\right]dx\\,dy \\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad=\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\int_x^y e^{g(t)}\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\n       -\\int_x^y g(t)\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\\right]dx\\,dy\n \\ \\geq\\ 0.\\label{weighted:scalar-identity}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-weighted-scalar",
          "exact_quote": "Let $b>0$, $\\lambda=\\pi/b$, and $$p_0(t)=\\frac{\\lambda^2}{\\sin^2(\\lambda t)},\\qquad 0<t<b.$$ Let $p:(0,b)\\to(0,\\infty)$ be continuous and suppose that $g=\\log(p/p_0)$ is bounded. Let $W$ be nonnegative and measurable on $(0,b)$, with $W(b-z)=W(z)$ almost everywhere and $W(z)/\\sin(\\lambda z)$ essentially bounded. Write $$\\Delta_b=\\{(x,y):0<x<y<b\\},\\qquad\n d\\nu_{xy}(t)=\\frac{p_0(t)\\,dt}{\\int_x^y p_0},\\quad x<t<y.$$ Then all the following integrals are finite, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\frac{\\int_x^y p}{\\int_x^y p_0}\n       -\\frac{g(x)+g(y)}2\\right]dx\\,dy \\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad=\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\int_x^y e^{g(t)}\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\n       -\\int_x^y g(t)\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\\right]dx\\,dy\n \\ \\geq\\ 0.\\label{weighted:scalar-identity}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-344.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $b>0$, $\\lambda=\\pi/b$, and $$p_0(t)=\\frac{\\lambda^2}{\\sin^2(\\lambda t)},\\qquad 0<t<b.$$ Let $p:(0,b)\\to(0,\\infty)$ be continuous and suppose that $g=\\log(p/p_0)$ is bounded. Let $W$ be nonnegative and measurable on $(0,b)$, with $W(b-z)=W(z)$ almost everywhere and $W(z)/\\sin(\\lambda z)$ essentially bounded. Write $$\\Delta_b=\\{(x,y):0<x<y<b\\},\\qquad\n d\\nu_{xy}(t)=\\frac{p_0(t)\\,dt}{\\int_x^y p_0},\\quad x<t<y.$$ Then all the following integrals are finite, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\frac{\\int_x^y p}{\\int_x^y p_0}\n       -\\frac{g(x)+g(y)}2\\right]dx\\,dy \\notag\\\\\n &\\qquad=\\int_{\\Delta_b}W(y-x)\n \\left[\\log\\int_x^y e^{g(t)}\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\n       -\\int_x^y g(t)\\,d\\nu_{xy}(t)\\right]dx\\,dy\n \\ \\geq\\ 0.\\label{weighted:scalar-identity}\n\\end{aligned}$$",

        "exact_quote": "In the normalized intermediate case of Proposition <a href=\"#geom:structure\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geom:structure\">5</a>, let $f(x,y)$ be the Jacobi determinant of any fixed unit-speed geodesic, as in Proposition <a href=\"#lag:determinant\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lag:determinant\">6</a>. Put $$\\mathcal R=(0,T)^2,\\qquad\n w(z)=(\\sin z)^m\\left|\\frac{\\sin(2z)}2\\right|^k\n \\quad(0\\leq z\\leq T).$$ Then $$\\int_{\\mathcal R}f(x,y)\\,dx\\,dy\n \\ \\geq\\ \\int_{\\mathcal R}w(|y-x|)\\,dx\\,dy.\n \\label{weighted:comparison}$$ Equality holds if and only if $f(x,y)=w(|y-x|)$ for every $(x,y)\\in[0,T]^2$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-geo-gysin",
        "exact_quote": "The manifolds $U$ and $B$ are simply connected. There is an integral class $v\\in H^4(B;\\mathbb Z)$ with $\\rho^*v=z$. For $2j<n$, the following is an integral basis: $$\\label{geo:low-basis}\n H^{2j}(B;\\mathbb Z):\\qquad\n e^{j-2a}v^a,\\qquad 0\\le a\\le\\lfloor j/2\\rfloor.$$ All odd cohomology of $B$ vanishes, and its even cohomology is free abelian. In particular $H^2(B;\\mathbb Z)=\\mathbb Ze$ and $H^4(B;\\mathbb Z)=\\mathbb Ze^2\\oplus\\mathbb Zv$. The rational cohomology ring is generated by $e,v$, and $$\\label{geo:total-betti}\n \\dim_{\\mathbb Q}H^*(B;\\mathbb Q)=2r(r+1).$$*\n\n\n\n*Multiplication by $e$ at the middle fits into an exact sequence $$\\label{geo:middle-sequence}\n 0\\longrightarrow H^{n-2}(B;\\mathbb Z)\n \\xrightarrow{\\ e\\smile\\ }H^n(B;\\mathbb Z)\n \\longrightarrow\\mathbb Z/(r+1)\\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently the integral bilinear form $$\\label{geo:middle-form}\n (a,b)\\longmapsto\\int_B eab,\\qquad a,b\\in H^{n-2}(B;\\mathbb Z),$$ has determinant of absolute value $r+1$ in any integral basis. The map $e\\smile:H^n(B;\\mathbb Z)\\to H^{n+2}(B;\\mathbb Z)$ is an isomorphism.",
        "source_id": "PM-344.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-geo-gysin",

The rational cohomology ring is generated by $e,v$, and $$\\label{geo:total-betti}\n \\dim_{\\mathbb Q}H^*(B;\\mathbb Q)=2r(r+1).$$*\n\n\n\n*Multiplication by $e$ at the middle fits into an exact sequence $$\\label{geo:middle-sequence}\n 0\\longrightarrow H^{n-2}(B;\\mathbb Z)\n \\xrightarrow{\\ e\\smile\\ }H^n(B;\\mathbb Z)\n \\longrightarrow\\mathbb Z/(r+1)\\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently the integral bilinear form $$\\label{geo:middle-form}\n (a,b)\\longmapsto\\int_B eab,\\qquad a,b\\in H^{n-2}(B;\\mathbb Z),$$ has determinant of absolute value $r+1$ in any integral basis. The map $e\\smile:H^n(B;\\mathbb Z)\\to H^{n+2}(B;\\mathbb Z)$ is an isomorphism.",

      "explanation": "The unit tangent bundle and the space of geodesics are simply connected, the low-degree integral cohomology of the latter has an explicit basis, and the middle pairing has determinant of absolute value one more than the quaternionic rank.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-geo-attachment",
        "exact_quote": "There is an oriented rank-three vector bundle $E\\to B$ and an attaching map $$f:S(E)\\longrightarrow M.$$ Let $p:S(E)\\to B$ and put $x=f^*u\\in H^4(S(E);\\mathbb Z)$. The following properties hold:*\n\n1.  *The pullback $\\rho^*E$ is the first negative bundle for the free-loop energy. It is isomorphic as a real vector bundle to the rank-three bundle $K\\to U$ with fiber $$K_w=\\{a\\in w^\\perp:\n              J_a(D)=0\\},\n           \\quad J_a(0)=0,\\quad J_a'(0)=a,$$ where $J_a$ is the normal Jacobi field along $\\gamma_w$.*\n\n\n2.  *Let $\\widetilde p:S(\\rho^*E)\\to U$ and $\\widehat\\rho:S(\\rho^*E)\\to S(E)$ be the pullback square. Then $$\\label{geo:attachment-pullback}\n           \\widehat\\rho^*x=\\widetilde p^*z.$$*\n\n\n3.  *After choosing the orientation of $E$, one has $$\\label{geo:primitive-boundary}\n           p_!x=e.$$ With either orientation its coefficient is $\\pm1$.*\n\n\n4.  *The actual negative bundle satisfies $$\\label{geo:negative-pontryagin}\n           \\rho^*p_1(E)=4z.$$",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Blaschke manifold** ([source](#sec:introduction)): We call $M$ a *Blaschke manifold* if it has positive dimension and $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$.
- **pointwise injectivity radius** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Here $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}_p(M,g)$ is the supremum of the radii on whose open tangent balls $\exp_p$ is a diffeomorphism onto its image.
- **space of normal Jacobi fields** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Let $\mathcal J$ be the real vector space of normal Jacobi fields along $\gamma$.
- **unit tangent contact form** ([source](#pm-h-26)): Let $\lambda$ be the unit tangent contact form, $$\lambda_w(\zeta)=\langle w,d\pi_w\zeta\rangle.$$
- **free loop space** ([source](#pm-h-27)): Write $\Lambda M=H^1(\mathbb R/T\mathbb Z,M)$ for the Sobolev free loop space, with rotation of the parameter as its circle action.
- **strict-convexity remainder function** ([source](#pm-h-17)): For $r>0$, the strictly convex function $$\Phi(r)=r-1-\log r$$ is nonnegative and vanishes exactly at $r=1$.
- **space of oriented geodesics** ([source](#geo:section)): Thus $B$ is the closed smooth manifold of oriented, unmarked geodesics, of dimension $2n-2$.
- **cut locus notation** ([source](#inc:section)): Write $C_p=\operatorname{Cut}(p)$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-cut-time** [claim](#stmt-geom-cut-time) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-cut-time): Use compactness and exponential injectivity on balls, then pass to the limit at the diameter. -> geodesics minimize exactly up to the diameter
- **step-structure** [claim](#stmt-geom-structure) / [proof](#stmt-geom-structure): Cite the classical Blaschke structure theory. -> closed geodesics, sphere fibration of the cut locus, model types
- **step-jacobi-determinant** [claim](#stmt-lag-determinant) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lag-determinant): Frame independence, skew-symmetry and periodicity, then a graph chart on the Lagrangian curve. -> determinant formula in a fixed graph chart
- **step-poles** [claim](#stmt-lag-poles) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lag-poles): Smooth frames dividing out the vanishing columns and reading off the residues. -> pole expansions of the chart matrix
- **step-profiles** [claim](#stmt-lag-profiles) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lag-profiles): Differentiate the expansions, take determinant roots and compare limits at the endpoints. -> bounded logarithmic ratios of the profiles
- **step-schur** [claim](#stmt-lag-schur) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lag-schur): Block congruence, completing the square and integration along folded endpoint paths. -> Schur complement bounds for either endpoint order
- **step-block-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lag-block-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lag-block-bounds): Variational formula for the determinant root and integral Minkowski inequality. -> scalar lower bounds for both determinant factors
- **step-weighted-scalar** [claim](#stmt-weighted-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-weighted-scalar): Direct integration of the chord identity, Fubini and Jensen's inequality. -> weighted logarithmic cancellation identity
- **step-geodesic-comparison** [claim](#stmt-weighted-geodesic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-weighted-geodesic): Apply the scalar identity to both factors with the four folded weights, then use strict convexity. -> integral comparison along each geodesic with equality case
- **step-polar** [claim](#stmt-rig-polar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-polar): Polar coordinates off the cut locus and reversal of geodesics. -> polar integrals equal the volume or twice the volume
- **step-volume-comparison** [claim](#stmt-thm-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-polar-2): Integrate the geodesic defect over the unit tangent bundle with Liouville measure; equality gives harmonicity and Szabo's classification. -> sharp volume lower bound with rigidity
- **step-volume-reduction** [claim](#stmt-thm-volume-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-polar-3): An upper bound plus the comparison gives equal volumes, so the equality case applies. -> reduction of rigidity to an upper volume bound
- **step-cut-incidence** [claim](#stmt-inc-normal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-normal): Constant-rank cut map, Leray-Hirsch presentation, self-intersection and spin representation arguments. -> normal four-plane bundle with its characteristic classes
- **step-pontryagin** [claim](#stmt-inc-pontryagin) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-pontryagin): Rank bound on the complement of two disjoint direction planes with freeness of the triple-point algebra. -> total Pontryagin class formula
- **step-signature** [claim](#stmt-sig-coefficient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sig-coefficient): Hirzebruch signature theorem, exact small ranks and a residue contour estimate. -> unit incidence coefficient
- **step-contact** [claim](#stmt-geo-contact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-contact): Integrate over circle fibers and compute the contact density. -> volume as a top Euler power, with model value
- **step-gysin** [claim](#stmt-geo-gysin) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-gysin): Integral Gysin sequences, universal coefficients and Poincare duality. -> cohomology lattice and middle determinant of the geodesic space
- **step-attachment** [claim](#stmt-geo-attachment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-attachment): Morse-Bott analysis of the free-loop energy, unit coefficient over every prime field, and identification of the negative bundle. -> attaching map with its class relations
- **step-parameters** [claim](#stmt-geo-parameters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-parameters): Leray-Hirsch decomposition over the sphere bundle and half-integrality of the base class. -> arithmetic parameters and moment determinant
- **step-power-relations** [claim](#stmt-alg-relations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-relations): Nilpotence of the attachment class and its antipodal image, then a nondegenerate moment pairing argument for nonvanishing. -> vanishing relations and nonzero parameter
- **step-complete-intersection** [claim](#stmt-alg-complete-intersection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-complete-intersection): Coprimality of the relations and exact sequences giving the Hilbert series. -> complete intersection algebra presenting the cohomology
- **step-top-functional** [claim](#stmt-alg-determinant) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-determinant): Explicit extraction algebra, perfect pairing, and evaluation of the determinant at a special parameter. -> normalization of the top functional
- **step-upper-bound** [claim](#stmt-alg-upper) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-upper): Termwise bound of the coefficient sum and explicit evaluation at the extremal formal parameters. -> sharp upper bound on the contact number
- **step-complex-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-rig-complex) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-complex): Homotopy equivalence with the model and Yang's volume theorem. -> rigidity in complex-projective type
- **step-planes-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-rig-planes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-planes): Diffeomorphism classification of projective planes and the equal-volume theorem for Zoll metrics. -> rigidity for the quaternionic and Cayley planes
- **step-quaternionic-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-rig-quaternionic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-quaternionic): Translate the contact number bound into a volume bound through the contact formula. -> rigidity in quaternionic type
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-blaschke) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-quaternionic-2): Assemble the cases by model type, using the sphere and real-projective cases and the three corollaries. -> metric Blaschke classification

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-model-number-binomial-difference**: For every r at least two, the model contact number equals the difference of two central-type binomial coefficients, and this difference equals the one-over-(2r+1) binomial expression used as the sharp upper bound.
- **inv-binomial-difference-evaluation**: The absolute value of the formal extremal evaluation is the difference of the two displayed binomial coefficients.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-rank-one-quaternionic-line**: Applying the bound formula to the quaternionic projective line or to non-quaternionic types. -> The bound is stated only for quaternionic type with r at least two; other types use different arguments.
- **anti-numeric-check-as-proof**: Treating the finite integer check of the model number identity as a proof of the volume bound for a given manifold. -> The check only confirms an arithmetic identity for the model; the inequality for a Blaschke manifold needs the topological argument.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The sharp bound involves an explicit finite integer constant; the code computes it as a difference of binomial coefficients and an independent oracle recomputes it from a factorial formula.

```python
# INPUTS: one integer r on stdin (r >= 2)
# PRECONDITIONS: r is an integer with r >= 2
# OUTPUTS: one integer on stdout, the model number for rank r
# TIME: polynomial in r bit operations for big-integer binomials
# SPACE: O(r) bits
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python big integers
# FAILURE: non-integer or empty input raises an exception and a nonzero exit
import sys
from math import comb

def main():
    r = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
    big = comb(4*r-2, 2*r-1)
    small = comb(4*r-2, 2*r-3)
    print(big - small)

main()
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The results are existence and rigidity theorems about closed Riemannian manifolds. They give no finite procedure that could be turned into a library or program. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Closed geodesics of one common period are a property of exact smooth metrics. Numerical meshes or discretised worlds do not satisfy the hypotheses, so no simulation guarantee follows. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The cohomological statements concern Gysin sequences of specific bundles. They do not define a verifiable tool input or output. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proofs rely on integral inequalities, characteristic classes and a Morse-theoretic attachment. None is a terminating computation with stated cost, so no algorithm is extracted. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper builds no storage or indexing structure. The cut-locus fibration is a continuous geometric object. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no arithmetic kernel, dataflow or numerical routine to map to hardware. The results are structural classifications. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol or security reduction appears. The topic is geometric rigidity. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorems require exact smooth closed manifolds with a global equality. They are not statements about learned or estimated manifolds, so no learning guarantee follows. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The volume bound is a geometric inequality among Blaschke manifolds. It is not an optimisation model with a feasible set or objective that a solver could use. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the statements involves message passing, consensus or replicated state. Discrete networks lack the smooth structure the theorems need. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main use is internal to differential geometry and topology, as a classification of Blaschke manifolds. It is not an engineering contract. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-closed-manifold-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The classification and the volume comparison are stated only for connected closed smooth Riemannian manifolds of positive dimension. Non-compact manifolds, manifolds with boundary, and non-smooth or merely discretised metrics are outside the statement. Blocked use: ['Applying the classification to non-compact or boundary-bearing manifolds', 'Applying the classification to meshes or sampled point clouds as if they were smooth closed manifolds']
- **limit-global-equality-hypothesis** (hypothesis): Everything rests on the global equality of injectivity radius and diameter. If the injectivity radius is strictly smaller than the diameter, the common minimizing property fails, and neither the volume comparison nor the rigidity conclusion is available. Blocked use: ['Using the volume lower bound for a metric whose injectivity radius is below its diameter', 'Concluding that a generic or perturbed metric is isometric to a model']
- **limit-intermediate-cases-only** (edge_case): The geodesic comparison is proved only in the normalized intermediate case, after the circle, sphere and real-projective cases have been separated off. The conjugate multiplicity at the cut time must lie strictly between zero and the full normal dimension for the block splitting to make sense. Blocked use: ['Applying the weighted geodesic comparison to the sphere or real-projective case', 'Reusing the two-block Schur factorization when one of the two blocks is empty']
- **limit-projective-line-excluded** (edge_case): The model list for the intermediate cases starts at rank two. The projective lines are not treated as intermediate cases, because they are already spheres. Blocked use: ['Reading the intermediate model table as covering rank-one projective lines']
- **limit-bounded-log-ratio** (hypothesis): The weighted scalar identity requires that the logarithm of the ratio of the profile to the reference profile be bounded, together with a reflection-symmetric nonnegative measurable weight. Profiles that blow up faster or slower than the reciprocal-square sine at the endpoints are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the scalar cancellation identity to profiles with unbounded logarithmic ratio', 'Applying it with a weight that is not symmetric under reflection of the interval']
- **limit-quaternionic-rank-two-plus** (hypothesis): The sharp upper bound on the contact characteristic number is stated for quaternionic-type manifolds of dimension 4r with r at least two. Complex-projective type, the Cayley plane and the sphere cases use other arguments, and the bound is not claimed for them. Blocked use: ['Applying the contact number bound to complex-projective or Cayley-plane type', 'Applying the bound with r equal to one']
- **limit-attachment-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The attaching map and the descended rank-three bundle come from a Morse-Bott disk-attachment theorem on an infinite-dimensional loop space. This yields existence and cohomological information only, not an explicit map that could be computed. Blocked use: ['Extracting an algorithm that builds the attaching map', 'Quoting a computational cost for the free-loop construction']
- **limit-equality-rigidity-external** (formal_scope): Metric rigidity in the equality case is obtained by showing the manifold is harmonic and then invoking an external classification of compact simply connected harmonic manifolds. The conclusion is only as strong as that cited theorem. Blocked use: ["Presenting the equality case as proved from the paper's own estimates alone", 'Applying the equality argument without simple connectivity']
- **limit-planes-cited-classification** (formal_scope): For the quaternionic and Cayley planes the argument imports a diffeomorphism classification of projective-plane-like manifolds and an equal-volume theorem for metrics with one common least geodesic period. These cited inputs are not re-proved in the paper. Blocked use: ["Treating the plane case as intrinsic to the paper's own volume bound", 'Using the plane argument for manifolds that are not Zoll with a common period']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the metric Blaschke conjecture: every connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold of positive dimension whose global injectivity radius equals its diameter is, up to scale, a standard compact rank-one symmetric space.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

On a round sphere, every geodesic minimizes from its starting point to the antipode, at a distance equal to the diameter. The standard real, complex and quaternionic projective spaces and the Cayley plane have the same common minimizing-distance property. The metric Blaschke conjecture asks whether this property characterizes these compact rank-one symmetric spaces.

For a connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold $(M,g)$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\inf_{p\in M}\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}_p(M,g),
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)=\sup_{p,q\in M}d_g(p,q).$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}_p(M,g)$ is the supremum of the radii on whose open tangent balls $\exp_p$ is a diffeomorphism onto its image. We call $M$ a *Blaschke manifold* if it has positive dimension and $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$. The global equality implies that every unit-speed geodesic minimizes precisely up to the common diameter; we give the short deduction in Lemma <a href="#geom:cut-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:cut-time">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-thm-blaschke"></a>
<div id="thm:blaschke" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (The metric Blaschke theorem). *Let $(M,g)$ be a connected closed smooth Riemannian manifold of positive dimension. If $\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$, then $(M,g)$ is isometric, up to multiplication of the metric by a positive constant, to a round sphere, a standard real, complex or quaternionic projective space, or the Cayley plane.*

</div>

The classification includes every quaternionic dimension. Its hypothesis is the global equality just defined. Classical Blaschke structure associates to $M$ a compact rank-one symmetric *model* $M_0$ by its cut-locus and integral cohomology type. Throughout the volume comparisons, the standard metric $g_0$ on $M_0$ is scaled to have the same diameter as $(M,g)$. Prime geodesics have length twice the diameter, so fixing the diameter is equivalent to fixing their prime period.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and its predecessors

Blaschke’s original question concerned surfaces with a common reconvergence property for geodesics. Green settled the surface problem (Blaschke 1921; Green 1963) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Blaschke1921](#ref-Blaschke1921), [Green1963](#ref-Green1963)]; the later geometric formulation and its development are described in Besse (1978) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Besse1978](#ref-Besse1978)] and in the corrected account of McKay (2016) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[McKay2016](#ref-McKay2016)]. The classical theory connects the minimizing condition to closed geodesics, smooth cut loci and fibrations of tangent spheres by great spheres. The topological restrictions of Bott and Samelson explain the appearance of the compact rank-one symmetric models (Besse 1978, Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Besse1978](#ref-Besse1978)].

The sphere and real-projective metric cases were established through the work of Berger, Kazdan, Weinstein and Yang; see Besse (1978, Appendices D–E) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Besse1978](#ref-Besse1978)] and McKay (2016, secs. 11–12) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[McKay2016](#ref-McKay2016)]. Berger’s sphere argument combines the geometric volume problem with Kazdan’s scalar integral inequality (Berger 1978; Kazdan 1978) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Berger1978AppendixD](#ref-Berger1978AppendixD), [Kazdan1978AppendixE](#ref-Kazdan1978AppendixE)]. Berger subsequently proved the sharp isoembolic inequality by a weighted comparison of Jacobi determinants and Jensen’s inequality (Berger 1980, 263–64) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Berger1980](#ref-Berger1980)]. These comparisons are central predecessors of the analytic part of the present proof.

Under additional hypotheses on families of totally geodesic round spheres of curvature $4$, Rovenskii and Toponogov (1996, Theorem 26, pp. 8–11) obtain a sharp projective volume comparison. Their Jacobi determinant splits into an exact curvature-$4$ factor and a complementary factor controlled by Kazdan’s inequality. Our fixed-chart Schur-complement argument uses the cut-time intersection pattern without requiring that geometric splitting.

For the projective models, it is useful to distinguish three kinds of information: topology, volume at a fixed geodesic period, and metric isometry. Yang proved that a Blaschke manifold of complex projective *homotopy type* has the model volume at the same prime geodesic period (Yang 1991, Theorem, p. 380) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Yang1991](#ref-Yang1991)]. For the projective planes, smooth incidence geometry and the classification of Kramer and Stolz (2007, Corollary C) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KramerStolz2007](#ref-KramerStolz2007)] identify the point manifold diffeomorphically. Wilking records the equal-volume theorem for a manifold homeomorphic to a compact rank-one symmetric space whose unit geodesics all have the same least period, attributing it to Reznikov (Wilking 2001, 282) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Wilking2001](#ref-Wilking2001)]. Reznikov’s earlier work on the quaternionic weak Blaschke conjecture (Reznikov 1985) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Reznikov1985HP](#ref-Reznikov1985HP)] is another predecessor of this volume approach. The homeomorphism hypothesis in the result recorded by Wilking, and the other hypotheses just stated, will be verified at their uses. In higher quaternionic dimensions our upper bound is intrinsic: it is proved from the Blaschke geometry and integral topology in Sections <a href="#inc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:section">6</a>–<a href="#alg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:section">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Two volume bounds and their common equality case

The first part of the proof establishes a comparison valid for every model type.

<a id="stmt-thm-comparison"></a>
<div id="thm:comparison" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Sharp volume comparison). *Every Blaschke manifold and its model at the same diameter satisfy $$\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M,g)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M_0,g_0).$$ Equality holds if and only if $(M,g)$ is isometric to $(M_0,g_0)$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-thm-volume-reduction"></a>
<div id="thm:volume-reduction" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Reduction to an upper volume bound). *If a Blaschke manifold has volume at most that of its model at the same diameter, then it is isometric to that model.*

</div>

After the circle, sphere and real-projective cases are separated, the analytic comparison in each remaining type starts along one closed geodesic. Normal Jacobi fields that vanish at a specified time form a Lagrangian plane in the symplectic space of Jacobi fields. A fixed graph chart separates the determinant of their endpoint pairing into a regular factor and a Schur-complement factor. The size of the Schur block is the dimension of the space of normal Jacobi fields vanishing at both the starting point and its cut point; the regular block has the complementary normal dimension. Both determinant factors can be bounded by scalar interval integrals, including when the actual endpoint order is reversed. This construction uses the cut-time intersection pattern; it does not require parallel eigenspaces of the curvature operator.

The resulting scalar estimates are combined by a logarithmic form of Kazdan’s weighted cancellation (Kazdan 1978, Appendix E, especially E.4–E.8) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kazdan1978AppendixE](#ref-Kazdan1978AppendixE)]. Its reflection symmetry cancels the integrated endpoint terms against interval averages. The scalar reference profiles are reciprocal-square sines, one on the full geodesic period and the other on a half-period. Folding the latter factor onto the two half-periods produces four weights, one for each choice of a half-period for each endpoint. Their equal, symmetric row and column sums allow both comparisons to contribute with the correct multiplicities. A nonnegative strict-convexity remainder records equality. Integrating over the unit tangent bundle gives the volume lower bound. Equality makes every radial volume density equal to its model value, and Szabó’s classification of compact simply connected harmonic manifolds then gives metric rigidity (Szabó 1990, 1988) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Szabo1990](#ref-Szabo1990), [Szabo1988](#ref-Szabo1988)].

The quaternionic upper bound has a different source. Fix dimension $4r$, with $r\ge2$. Pairs of points at the cut distance form a smooth incidence manifold. Its actual normal four-plane bundle identifies the great-three-sphere families of tangent directions. Two different direction planes at one point intersect only at zero. The rank of their complement forces a formula for the tangent Pontryagin class; the signature theorem and a uniform estimate in every rank then determine its coefficient. Characteristic-class and signature theory enter here in their usual integral and oriented forms (J. W. Milnor and Stasheff 1974) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MilnorStasheff1974](#ref-MilnorStasheff1974)].

Next consider the space of oriented geodesics, forgetting the marked starting point. The period-normalized contact form expresses volume as a top power of the Euler class of its geodesic-circle bundle. Integral Gysin sequences first determine the lattice of classes and the determinant of a middle pairing. The first negative bundle of the free-loop energy transfers the incidence coefficient to this space. This step uses the fixed-endpoint Morse index theorem of J. Milnor (1963) and the equivariant negative-bundle attachment developed in closed-geodesic Morse theory; we use the formulation of Ottosen and Bökstedt (2007, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[OttosenBokstedt2007](#ref-OttosenBokstedt2007)], following Klingenberg (1978) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Klingenberg1978](#ref-Klingenberg1978)]. The required primitive attaching coefficient is established over every prime field. The attaching map from the sphere bundle of the descended negative bundle to $M$ also pulls back its cohomology generator; the vanishing of the $(r+1)$st power supplies the relations for the final algebraic calculation. Finally, a complete-intersection calculation turns the middle determinant into the sharp upper volume bound. Rational cohomology alone would not retain the arithmetic that normalizes this bound.

Section <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">2</a> fixes the geometric conventions. Sections <a href="#sec:lagrangian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lagrangian">3</a> and <a href="#weighted:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:section">4</a> prove the geodesic comparison, and Section <a href="#rig:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rig:section">5</a> completes its global volume and equality argument. Sections <a href="#inc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:section">6</a> and <a href="#sig:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sig:section">7</a> establish the quaternionic characteristic class. Sections <a href="#geo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:section">8</a> and <a href="#alg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:section">9</a> convert it to the upper bound. Section <a href="#completion:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="completion:section">10</a> checks the remaining classical inputs and completes the metric classification.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Recent related rigidity results

There are also rigidity theorems with additional geometric hypotheses. Shankar, Spatzier and Wilking treat an upper sectional-curvature bound (Shankar, Spatzier, and Wilking 2005, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ShankarSpatzierWilking2005](#ref-ShankarSpatzierWilking2005)], and Su, Sun and Wang prove the corresponding normalized lower-curvature result (Su, Sun, and Wang 2016) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[SuSunWang2016](#ref-SuSunWang2016)]. Connell, Islam, Nguyen and Spatzier obtain rigidity under curvature bounds and agreement with a model away from a prescribed convex set (Connell et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[ConnellIslamNguyenSpatzier2026](#ref-ConnellIslamNguyenSpatzier2026)].

Song’s September 2026 preprint proves Blaschke metric rigidity assuming an adapted complex structure on the entire tangent bundle (Song 2026, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Song2026](#ref-Song2026)]. This entire-extension hypothesis is additional to the Blaschke condition.

<a id="geom:section"></a>
### Blaschke geometry and model normalization

<a id="stmt-geom-cut-time"></a>
<div id="geom:cut-time" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *Let $D=\mathop{\mathrm{inj}}(M,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(M,g)$. Every unit-speed geodesic segment of length at most $D$ is minimizing. A segment of length less than $D$ is the unique minimizing segment between its endpoints, and no segment of length greater than $D$ is minimizing.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-cut-time"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-cut-time</summary>

*Proof.* Compactness and positive dimension give $0<D<\infty$, and every pointwise injectivity radius is at least $D$. For $0<t<D$, the exponential map at a point $p$ is injective and nonsingular on a tangent ball containing the sphere of radius $t$. A minimizing geodesic to $\exp_p(tv)$ exists by completeness and has length at most $t$. Its initial vector lies in that ball and maps to the same endpoint as $tv$, so injectivity identifies the two vectors. This proves both minimality and uniqueness. Taking $t\uparrow D$ and using continuity of distance proves minimality at $D$. If a segment of length greater than $D$ minimized, the distance between its endpoints would exceed the diameter. ◻

</details>

In dimension one, a connected closed manifold is a circle. Arclength identifies its metric with a round circle of the same length. We henceforth suppose $n=\dim M\ge2$.

We use the following classical structure conclusions (Besse 1978, vol. 93, chap. 5 and 7) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Besse1978](#ref-Besse1978)]. Precise statements in the corrected survey are McKay (2016, sec. 4, Corollary 1, Theorem 1, and Sections 7, 11–12) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[McKay2016](#ref-McKay2016)].

<a id="stmt-geom-structure"></a>
<div id="geom:structure" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Classical Blaschke structure). *Every unit-speed geodesic is simply closed with period $2D$. For each $p\in M$, the map $v\mapsto\exp_p(Dv)$ from $S_pM$ onto the cut locus is a smooth sphere fibration whose fibers are great spheres of one dimension $k$, independent of $p$. Its differential has nullity $k$. The model is determined as follows:*

1.  *If $k=n-1$, the model is a sphere; if $k=0$, it is a real projective space. In these two cases the metric classification is known: the manifold is isometric to its equally scaled model.*

2.  *If $0<k<n-1$, the manifold is simply connected and its integral cohomology ring has one generator of degree $k+1$. The possibilities are complex-projective type, quaternionic-projective type, and Cayley-plane type, with the dimensions listed below.*

</div>

The common period is the prime period, and each geodesic is simple. Indeed, if two distinct points of its parameter circle represented the same point of $M$, one of the two arcs between them would have length at most $D$ and positive length. Lemma <a href="#geom:cut-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:cut-time">4</a> would make that arc minimizing between equal endpoints, a contradiction. This also explains the freeness of the period action used later.

The simple-connectivity assertion in the intermediate case also has a short topological explanation. The homotopy sequence of $S^k\to S^{n-1}\to\operatorname{Cut}(p)$ gives $\pi_1(\operatorname{Cut}(p))=0$: the total sphere is simply connected and the fiber is connected. The exponential map identifies $M$ with the space obtained by attaching a closed $n$-ball to its cut locus along this fibration. Indeed, it gives a continuous bijection from that compact quotient to the Hausdorff manifold $M$, hence a homeomorphism. Attaching this $n$-cell does not change the fundamental group, since the intermediate cases have $n\ge4$.

<a id="geom:normalization"></a>
Only the intermediate cases require the new comparison. From now on rescale the metric so that $$\label{geom:normalization}
 D=\frac\pi2,\qquad T=2D=\pi,\qquad d=n-1,\qquad m=d-k.$$ Both $m$ and $k$ are positive. The intermediate model data are $$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
\text{model}&n&k&m\\ \hline
\mathbb C\mathrm P^r\ (r\ge2)&2r&1&2r-2\\
\mathbb H\mathrm P^r\ (r\ge2)&4r&3&4r-4\\
\mathbb O\mathrm P^2&16&7&8
\end{array}$$ The projective lines are already included in the sphere case.

On a standard model at this scale, the normal radial curvature operator has parallel eigenspaces of dimensions $m$ and $k$, with eigenvalues $1$ and $4$. Solving the scalar Jacobi equations with initial value zero and derivative one gives the polar density <a id="geom:model-density"></a>$$\label{geom:model-density}
 w(t)=(\sin t)^m\left|\frac{\sin2t}{2}\right|^k,
 \qquad 0\le t\le T.$$ It is positive away from $0,D,T$ and satisfies $w(T-t)=w(t)$. Let $\sigma_d=|S^d|$ denote ordinary unit-sphere area. The model volume is <a id="geom:model-volume"></a>$$\label{geom:model-volume}
 V_0=\sigma_d\int_0^D w(t)\,dt.$$ The eigenspace description is used only to calculate this model quantity. No curvature splitting or curvature bound is imposed on $M$.

Write $U=SM$, let $\phi_t$ be its geodesic flow, and use Liouville measure with unnormalized angular area: <a id="geom:liouville"></a>$$\label{geom:liouville}
 d\mu(\xi)=d\operatorname{vol}_g(p)\,d\sigma_p(\xi),
 \qquad \xi\in S_pM.$$ This measure is invariant under the flow and has full support; if $V=\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M,g)$, then $\mu(U)=\sigma_d V$. Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a> gives $\phi_T=\mathrm{Id}_U$. Below, all determinants of Jacobi tensors are taken in absolute value, including beyond the cut time.

<a id="sec:lagrangian"></a>
### A Lagrangian comparison along a closed geodesic

We work in the intermediate case $$D=\frac{\pi}{2},\qquad T=2D=\pi,\qquad
 d=n-1,\qquad 0<k<d,\qquad m=d-k.$$ Fix a unit-speed geodesic $\gamma:\mathbb R\to M$. The structural conclusions in Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a> give the common period $T$, the absence of conjugate points at separations in $(0,D)$, and conjugate multiplicity $k$ at separation $D$. This section uses these geometric conclusions and the Jacobi equation. Its comparison requires no assumption about the volume of $M$.

The purpose of the construction is to separate a Jacobi determinant into two factors with different scalar comparison profiles. Unlike the curvature-invariant splitting supplied by the totally geodesic curvature-four sphere families of Rovenskii and Toponogov (1996, Theorem 26) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[RovenskiiToponogov1996](#ref-RovenskiiToponogov1996)], this separation takes place in a fixed coordinate chart on the space of Jacobi fields. No splitting of the curvature operator is assumed.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The symplectic curve of vanishing Jacobi fields

We use the classical symplectic description of Jacobi fields; see, for example, Zelditch (2017, sec. 2.1.2 and 10.5.2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Zelditch2017Notes](#ref-Zelditch2017Notes)] and the variational-index framework of Duistermaat (1976) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Duistermaat1976](#ref-Duistermaat1976)]. We derive the required identities to fix the signs and the normalization of the endpoint pairing.

Let $\mathcal J$ be the real vector space of normal Jacobi fields along $\gamma$. A prime on a field denotes covariant differentiation along $\gamma$. Prescribing the normal pair $(J(t),J'(t))$ at one time identifies $\mathcal J$ with a vector space of dimension $2d$. The form <a id="eq:lag-wronskian"></a>$$\omega(I,J)=\langle I,J'\rangle-\langle I',J\rangle
 \label{eq:lag-wronskian}$$ is independent of the evaluation time: its derivative vanishes by the Jacobi equation and the self-adjointness of $X\mapsto R(X,\dot\gamma)\dot\gamma$. Evaluation of the initial data identifies it with the standard nondegenerate symplectic form.

<a id="eq:lag-velocity"></a>
For each $t\in\mathbb R$, set $$L_t=\{J\in\mathcal J:J(t)=0\}.$$ The Jacobi initial-value problem shows that $L_t$ depends smoothly on $t$, has dimension $d$, and is isotropic for $\omega$. It is therefore Lagrangian. Its motion carries a natural positive inner product. For $I,J\in L_t$, choose a smooth local section $J_s\in L_s$ with $J_t=J$, and define $$G_t(I,J)=\omega(I,\dot J_t),
 \qquad
 \dot J_t=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=t}J_s\in\mathcal J.$$ The definition is independent of the extension: a section of $L_s$ vanishing as an element of $\mathcal J$ at $s=t$ has derivative in $L_t$, which pairs to zero with $I$. Differentiating $J_s(s)=0$ gives $\dot J_t(t)=-J'(t)$. Since $I(t)=0$, our sign convention yields $$G_t(I,J)=\langle I'(t),J'(t)\rangle.
 \label{eq:lag-velocity}$$ Thus $G_t$ is symmetric and positive definite, and $J\mapsto J'(t)$ is an isometry from $(L_t,G_t)$ to the normal space $\dot\gamma(t)^\perp$.

<a id="eq:lag-periodicity"></a>
We will need periodicity of the fields themselves. For any normal initial data $(a,b)$ at time zero, the connection splitting of $T(SM)$ realizes $(a,b)$ as the derivative of a smooth curve of unit initial velocities. The associated variation through unit-speed geodesics has Jacobi field with initial data $(a,b)$; it is normal because its tangential component and its derivative vanish initially. All geodesics in this variation satisfy the same identity $\phi_T=\mathrm{Id}_{SM}$. Differentiating that identity, or differentiating the equality of the varied positions and velocities after time $T$, gives $$J(t+T)=J(t),\qquad J'(t+T)=J'(t)
 \quad(J\in\mathcal J).
 \label{eq:lag-periodicity}$$ These are equalities in the same tangent space, since $\gamma(t+T)=\gamma(t)$. They do not assert that parallel transport around $\gamma$ is the identity. In particular, $$(L_{t+T},G_{t+T})=(L_t,G_t).$$

<a id="eq:lag-intersections"></a>
The intersection pattern is consequently $$\dim(L_x\cap L_y)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&0<y-x<T,\quad y-x\ne D,\\
  k,&y-x=D.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:lag-intersections}$$ For $0<y-x<D$, this is exponential nonsingularity. At separation $D$, evaluation at $y=x+D$ of the fields vanishing at $x$ identifies the kernel with the kernel of the angular differential of the cut-locus fibration, whose dimension is $k$. If $D<y-x<T$, a field in the intersection also vanishes at $x+T$ by <a href="#eq:lag-periodicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-periodicity">[eq:lag-periodicity]</a>; the separation from $y$ to $x+T$ is less than $D$, so the field is zero.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The determinant and a fixed graph chart

<a id="stmt-lag-determinant"></a>
<div id="lag:determinant" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Jacobi determinant normalization). *Choose $G_x$- and $G_y$-orthonormal frames $Z_x=(Z_{x,1},\ldots,Z_{x,d})$ and $Z_y=(Z_{y,1},\ldots,Z_{y,d})$ of $L_x$ and $L_y$. Then <a id="eq:lag-f"></a>$$f(x,y)=\left|\det\bigl(\omega(Z_{x,a},Z_{y,b})\bigr)_{a,b=1}^d\right|
 \label{eq:lag-f}$$ is independent of these choices. It is continuous, symmetric in its arguments, and separately $T$-periodic. It is the absolute normal Jacobi determinant at $y$ of fields vanishing at $x$ with orthonormal initial derivatives there. In particular, for $0<y-x<D$ it is the polar volume density with respect to $dt$ and ordinary sphere area at $\gamma(x)$.*

<a id="eq:lag-graph-determinant"></a>
*Use the same orthonormal basis of $\dot\gamma(0)^\perp$ for the coordinates $$(q,p)=(J(0),J'(0))\in\mathbb R^d\oplus\mathbb R^d,$$ choosing its first $k$ vectors to be the initial derivatives of a basis of $L_0\cap L_D$. On $$\mathcal I=(0,D)\cup(D,T)$$ there is a smooth symmetric matrix $A(t)$ such that $L_t=\{(q,A(t)q):q\in\mathbb R^d\}$, and $A'(t)>0$. For $x,y\in\mathcal I$, $$f(x,y)=
 \frac{|\det(A(y)-A(x))|}
 {\sqrt{\det A'(x)\det A'(y)}}.
 \label{eq:lag-graph-determinant}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lag-determinant"><summary>Proof of stmt-lag-determinant</summary>

*Proof.* Changing either orthonormal frame multiplies the pairing matrix by an orthogonal matrix. This leaves its absolute determinant unchanged. Local smooth orthonormal frames exist, so $f$ is continuous even when the pairing is singular. Skew-symmetry of $\omega$ replaces the matrix by its negative transpose when the arguments are exchanged, proving symmetry. Periodicity follows from <a href="#eq:lag-periodicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-periodicity">[eq:lag-periodicity]</a> and <a href="#eq:lag-velocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-velocity">[eq:lag-velocity]</a>.

Evaluation of the pairing at $y$ gives $$\omega(Z_{x,a},Z_{y,b})
 =\langle Z_{x,a}(y),Z'_{y,b}(y)\rangle.$$ The vectors $Z'_{y,b}(y)$ form an orthonormal normal basis, and the fields $Z_{x,a}$ have zero values and orthonormal derivatives at $x$. This proves the claimed determinant interpretation. More explicitly, an angular variation of a unit direction with derivative $e$ gives the Jacobi field $J$ with $J(x)=0$ and $J'(x)=e$. Its value at $x+r$ is the angular derivative of $\exp_{\gamma(x)}(r\,\cdot)$. Together with the unit radial derivative and Gauss’s lemma, this gives the polar density $f(x,x+r)\,dr\,d\sigma$. There is no additional power of $r$ in this polar formula; the Cartesian normal-coordinate density would be $f(x,x+r)/r^d$.

In the stated coordinates, $$\omega((q,p),(\widetilde q,\widetilde p))
 =q^{\mathsf T}\widetilde p-p^{\mathsf T}\widetilde q,
