 \qquad L_0=\{q=0\}.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:lag-intersections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-intersections">[eq:lag-intersections]</a> makes $L_t$ transverse to $L_0$ for $t\in\mathcal I$, proving the graph representation. Isotropy is equivalent to $A=A^{\mathsf T}$. For the graph frame $E_tq=(q,A(t)q)$, $$G_t(E_tq,E_tr)=q^{\mathsf T}A'(t)r,
 \qquad
 \omega(E_xq,E_yr)=q^{\mathsf T}(A(y)-A(x))r.$$ Positivity of $G_t$ gives $A'(t)>0$. The frames $E_tA'(t)^{-1/2}$ are orthonormal for $G_t$, so taking determinants in the last identity proves <a href="#eq:lag-graph-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-graph-determinant">[eq:lag-graph-determinant]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:lag-blocks"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-velocity-factorization"></a>
The coordinate basis is now fixed. Split both $q$ and $p$ into their first $k$ and last $m$ coordinates and write $$A(t)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  V_b(t)&C(t)\\ C(t)^{\mathsf T}&H(t)
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 S(t)=V_b'(t)-C'(t)H'(t)^{-1}C'(t)^{\mathsf T}.
 \label{eq:lag-blocks}$$ The subscript in $V_b$ distinguishes this matrix from the volume of the manifold. Here $H$ is an $m\times m$ matrix and $S$ is a $k\times k$ matrix. Positive definiteness of $A'$ and block elimination give $$H'(t)>0,\qquad S(t)>0,\qquad
 \det A'(t)=\det H'(t)\det S(t)
 \quad(t\in\mathcal I).
 \label{eq:lag-velocity-factorization}$$

For comparison, on the standard model the adapted chart has $$A_{\mathrm{mod}}(t)
 =\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl(-2\cot(2t)\mathrm{Id}_k,-\cot(t)\mathrm{Id}_m\bigr).$$ Thus $H'_{\mathrm{mod}}(t)=\csc^2(t)\mathrm{Id}_m$ and $S_{\mathrm{mod}}(t)=4\csc^2(2t)\mathrm{Id}_k$. These formulas motivate the scalar reference profiles below.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The chart poles and determinant-root profiles

The curve $L_t$ remains smooth at $0,D,T$; it is the projection from $L_t$ to the fixed $q$-coordinate space that ceases to be invertible. Its kernel has dimension $d$ at $0,T$ and dimension $k$ at $D$. The following expansion shows how this loss of transversality enters $A$: at $D$ the pole lies entirely in the first $k$ coordinates, while the complementary block $H$ remains regular. Smooth remainders are needed because the comparison uses derivatives of the graph matrices.

<a id="stmt-lag-poles"></a>
<div id="lag:poles" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lag-middle-pole"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-end-poles"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Pole expansions). *There is a positive definite $k\times k$ matrix $B$ such that the graph matrix has the expansions $$\begin{aligned}
 A(t)&=-\frac1{t-D}
 \begin{pmatrix}B&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}+R_D(t),
 &&\text{near }D,\label{eq:lag-middle-pole}\\
 A(t)&=-\frac1{t-t_0}\mathrm{Id}_d+R_{t_0}(t),
 &&\text{near }t_0=0,T,\label{eq:lag-end-poles}
\end{aligned}$$ where each remainder extends smoothly across its indicated point. In particular, $C$ and $H$ extend smoothly across $D$, and $H'(D)>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lag-poles"><summary>Proof of stmt-lag-poles</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\tau=t-D$. Choose a local smooth frame of $L_t$, written as the columns of $$F(t)=\begin{pmatrix}X(t)\\P(t)\end{pmatrix},$$ whose first $k$ columns at $D$ are the vertical vectors $(0,e_a)$ for $1\le a\le k$. These are exactly the chosen coordinate basis of $L_0\cap L_D$. Isotropy with these vectors forces every vector in the image of $X(D)$ to have its first $k$ coordinates zero. The kernel of the value projection on $L_D$ is $L_0\cap L_D$, so that image has dimension $m$ and is the entire last coordinate space. Choose the remaining frame columns so that their value projections give a basis of this image. In blocks, their last $m$ value coordinates form an invertible matrix $M$.

The first $k$ columns of $X(D)$ vanish. Dividing these columns by $\tau$ gives a smooth matrix $\widetilde X(t)$ satisfying $$X(t)=\widetilde X(t)
 \begin{pmatrix}\tau\mathrm{Id}_k&0\\0&\mathrm{Id}_m\end{pmatrix}.$$ Let $G$ be the matrix of $G_D$ on the first $k$ frame vectors. For $1\le a,b\le k$, the definition of the velocity form in the fixed coordinates gives $$G_{ab}=\omega((0,e_a),\dot F_b(D))
 =-e_a^{\mathsf T}X_b'(D).$$ Consequently <a id="eq:lag-divided-frame"></a>$$\widetilde X(D)=
 \begin{pmatrix}-G&0\\N&M\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad G>0,\quad M\text{ invertible},
 \label{eq:lag-divided-frame}$$ for some matrix $N$. In particular $\widetilde X(D)$ is invertible. For $\tau\ne0$ sufficiently small, $$A(t)=P(t)
 \begin{pmatrix}\tau^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_k&0\\0&\mathrm{Id}_m\end{pmatrix}
 \widetilde X(t)^{-1}.$$ To identify its residue, let $P_1(t)$ denote the first $k$ columns of $P(t)$ and let $U_1(t)$ denote the first $k$ rows of $\widetilde X(t)^{-1}$. Then the only possibly singular summand is $\tau^{-1}P_1(t)U_1(t)$. At $D$, $$P_1(D)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k\\0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad U_1(D)=\begin{pmatrix}-G^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ Their product is $-\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(G^{-1},0)$. The difference $P_1(t)U_1(t)-P_1(D)U_1(D)$ divided by $\tau$ is smooth, as is the summand from the other frame columns. This proves <a href="#eq:lag-middle-pole" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-middle-pole">[eq:lag-middle-pole]</a> with $B=G^{-1}>0$, including the absence of any off-diagonal residue.

For fixed $z\in\mathbb R^m$, the graph vectors $$E_z(t)=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\z\end{pmatrix},
                 \begin{pmatrix}C(t)z\\H(t)z\end{pmatrix}\right)$$ extend smoothly through $D$. Their limits lie in $L_D$ by continuity of $L_t$, and are nonzero if $z\ne0$. The velocity identity in the fixed symplectic coordinates, now also at $D$, gives $$z^{\mathsf T}H'(D)z
 =\omega(E_z(D),\dot E_z(D))
 =G_D(E_z(D),E_z(D))>0.$$ This proves strict positivity of the extended derivative.

At $t_0=0$, take a frame whose value at $0$ is the whole vertical coordinate basis. All $X$-columns then vanish at $0$. The velocity metric on this basis is $\mathrm{Id}_d$ by <a href="#eq:lag-velocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-velocity">[eq:lag-velocity]</a>; hence $X'(0)=-\mathrm{Id}_d$ and $P(0)=\mathrm{Id}_d$. Dividing all value columns by $t$ and repeating the preceding calculation gives residue $-\mathrm{Id}_d$. At $t_0=T$, periodicity identifies both $L_T$ and its velocity metric with their values at zero in these same fixed coordinates. The same argument gives residue $-\mathrm{Id}_d$ there, proving <a href="#eq:lag-end-poles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-end-poles">[eq:lag-end-poles]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lag-profiles"></a>
<div id="lag:profiles" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8** (Scalar profiles). *Define <a id="eq:lag-profiles"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 h(t)&=\det(H'(t))^{1/m} &&(0<t<T),\\
 s(t)&=\det(S(t))^{1/k} &&(t\in\mathcal I),\\
 s_i(t)&=s(t+iD) &&(0<t<D,\ i=0,1).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:lag-profiles}$$ The function $h$ is smooth and positive across $D$. There are positive constants $c_H,C_H,c_i,C_i$, depending on the fixed geodesic and its chart, such that <a id="eq:lag-h-bounds"></a><a id="eq:lag-s-bounds"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 c_H&\le h(t)\sin^2t\le C_H &&(0<t<T),
 \label{eq:lag-h-bounds}\\
 c_i&\le \frac{s_i(t)\sin^2(2t)}4\le C_i
 &&(0<t<D,\ i=0,1).
 \label{eq:lag-s-bounds}
\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the logarithms of the ratios of $h$ to $1/\sin^2t$ and of $s_i$ to $4/\sin^2(2t)$ are bounded.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lag-profiles"><summary>Proof of stmt-lag-profiles</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lag:poles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:poles">7</a> and <a href="#eq:lag-velocity-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-velocity-factorization">[eq:lag-velocity-factorization]</a> show that $H'$ is positive throughout $(0,T)$, including $D$. Near $t_0=0,T$, put $\tau=t-t_0$. Differentiating the smooth-remainder expansion gives $$H'(t)=\tau^{-2}\mathrm{Id}_m+O(1),\qquad
 V_b'(t)=\tau^{-2}\mathrm{Id}_k+O(1),\qquad C'(t)=O(1).$$ Here and below $O(1)$ denotes a matrix bounded in operator norm as the indicated time is approached. Since $H'(t)^{-1}=O(\tau^2)$, the Schur complement satisfies <a id="eq:lag-S-end"></a>$$S(t)=\tau^{-2}\mathrm{Id}_k+O(1)\qquad(t\to0,T).
 \label{eq:lag-S-end}$$ At $D$, both $C'$ and $H'$ are smooth and $H'(D)$ is invertible. The middle pole therefore yields <a id="eq:lag-S-middle"></a>$$S(t)=(t-D)^{-2}B+O(1)\qquad(t\to D).
 \label{eq:lag-S-middle}$$ Taking determinant roots gives the positive limits $$\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &\text{left endpoint}&\text{right endpoint}\\ \hline
 h(t)\sin^2t\quad(0<t<T)&1&1\\
 s_0(t)\sin^2(2t)/4\quad(0<t<D)&1&\det(B)^{1/k}\\
 s_1(t)\sin^2(2t)/4\quad(0<t<D)&\det(B)^{1/k}&1.
 \end{array}$$ Each ratio thus extends continuously and positively to its compact closed interval. Its minimum and maximum supply the stated bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Schur complements for arbitrary endpoint order

The Schur complement and determinant-root concavity are standard matrix tools (Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004, Appendix A.5.5 and Exercises 3.18(b), 3.26(b)) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BoydVandenberghe2004](#ref-BoydVandenberghe2004)]. Here the essential point is to apply the factorization with either actual endpoint order. We retain the matrix calculation so that its signs and integration paths are explicit.

<a id="eq:lag-H-difference"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-Q"></a>
For distinct $x,y\in\mathcal I$, use $\Delta$ to denote the difference of the values at $y$ and $x$. Since $H$ is smooth and $H'>0$ throughout $(0,T)$, $$\Delta H=H(y)-H(x)=\int_x^y H'(t)\,dt
 \label{eq:lag-H-difference}$$ is positive definite for $y>x$ and negative definite for $y<x$. In particular it is invertible in either order. Define $$Q(x,y)=\Delta V_b-(\Delta C)(\Delta H)^{-1}(\Delta C)^{\mathsf T}.
 \label{eq:lag-Q}$$ Thus $Q$ is the Schur complement of the regular block in the endpoint difference $\Delta A$, whereas $S$ in <a href="#eq:lag-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-blocks">[eq:lag-blocks]</a> is the Schur complement in the velocity $A'$. The next proposition relates them by differentiating in either endpoint; the diagonal and cut-pair zeros of $Q$ then anchor the two interval bounds.

<a id="stmt-lag-schur"></a>
<div id="lag:schur" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:lag-Schur-factorization"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-Q-zeros"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-Q-derivatives"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-Q-bound-j"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-Q-bound-i"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Two Schur-complement bounds). *The matrix $Q(x,y)$ is symmetric and satisfies $$\det(\Delta A)=\det(\Delta H)\det Q(x,y),\qquad
 Q(y,x)=-Q(x,y).
 \label{eq:lag-Schur-factorization}$$ It extends continuously by zero to $y=x\in\mathcal I$, and $$Q(u,u+D)=Q(u+D,u)=0\qquad(0<u<D).
 \label{eq:lag-Q-zeros}$$ Whenever its distinct endpoints remain in $\mathcal I$, it obeys the positive-semidefinite matrix inequalities $$\partial_yQ(x,y)\ge S(y),\qquad
 -\partial_xQ(x,y)\ge S(x).
 \label{eq:lag-Q-derivatives}$$ Consequently, for every $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\in\{0,1\}$, $$\begin{aligned}
 Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\ge\int_u^v S(t+jD)\,dt,
 \label{eq:lag-Q-bound-j}\\
 Q(u+iD,v+jD)&\ge\int_u^v S(t+iD)\,dt.
 \label{eq:lag-Q-bound-i}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, this $Q$ is positive definite for all four choices of $i,j$, including $i=1,j=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lag-schur"><summary>Proof of stmt-lag-schur</summary>

*Proof.* With $R=(\Delta C)(\Delta H)^{-1}$, block congruence gives <a id="eq:lag-block-congruence"></a>$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k&-R\\0&\mathrm{Id}_m\end{pmatrix}
 (\Delta A)
 \begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k&0\\-R^{\mathsf T}&\mathrm{Id}_m\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}Q&0\\0&\Delta H\end{pmatrix}.
 \label{eq:lag-block-congruence}$$ This proves the determinant identity and identifies the nullity of $\Delta A$ with that of $Q$. Exchanging the endpoints changes the sign of each difference, and hence changes $Q$ to $-Q$.

<a id="eq:lag-Q-diagonal"></a>
For a matched pair $(u,u+D)$, the kernel of $\Delta A$ parametrizes $L_u\cap L_{u+D}$ by the common $q$ coordinate. It has dimension $k$ by <a href="#eq:lag-intersections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-intersections">[eq:lag-intersections]</a>. The matrix $Q$ has size $k\times k$ and therefore has full nullity: it is the zero matrix. This proves <a href="#eq:lag-Q-zeros" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-zeros">[eq:lag-Q-zeros]</a>, also in the reversed order. Near a diagonal point within the chart, Taylor expansion gives $$\Delta H=(y-x)H'(x)+O((y-x)^2),\quad
 \Delta C=(y-x)C'(x)+O((y-x)^2),$$ and the corresponding expansion for $\Delta V_b$. Invertibility of $H'(x)$ then gives $$Q(x,y)=(y-x)S(x)+O((y-x)^2).
 \label{eq:lag-Q-diagonal}$$ This establishes the zero extension needed at the endpoints of the integrations below.

Direct differentiation of <a href="#eq:lag-Q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q">[eq:lag-Q]</a>, using $\partial_y(\Delta H)^{-1}=-(\Delta H)^{-1}H'(y)(\Delta H)^{-1}$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \partial_yQ
 &=V_b'(y)-C'(y)R^{\mathsf T}-R C'(y)^{\mathsf T}
                  +R H'(y)R^{\mathsf T}\\
 &=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k&-R\end{pmatrix}A'(y)
                  \begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k\\-R^{\mathsf T}\end{pmatrix}.
\end{aligned}$$ The identical calculation with $-\partial_x$ gives the same expression with $A'(x)$. For either endpoint $a=x$ or $a=y$, completing the square gives the exact identity <a id="eq:lag-square-completion"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k&-R\end{pmatrix}A'(a)
                  \begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_k\\-R^{\mathsf T}\end{pmatrix}
 \notag\\
 &\quad=S(a)+
 \bigl(R-C'(a)H'(a)^{-1}\bigr)H'(a)
 \bigl(R-C'(a)H'(a)^{-1}\bigr)^{\mathsf T}.
 \label{eq:lag-square-completion}
\end{aligned}$$ The second summand is positive semidefinite. This proves <a href="#eq:lag-Q-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-derivatives">[eq:lag-Q-derivatives]</a>. The calculation used invertibility of $\Delta H$, but did not require its positive definiteness.

To obtain <a href="#eq:lag-Q-bound-j" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-bound-j">[eq:lag-Q-bound-j]</a>, fix the first endpoint $u+iD$ and move the second from $u+jD$ to $v+jD$. The starting value of $Q$ is zero by the diagonal extension if $i=j$, and by <a href="#eq:lag-Q-zeros" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-zeros">[eq:lag-Q-zeros]</a> otherwise. Integrating $\partial_yQ\ge S(y)$ on this interval gives the result. For <a href="#eq:lag-Q-bound-i" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-bound-i">[eq:lag-Q-bound-i]</a>, fix the second endpoint $v+jD$ and move the first from $u+iD$ to $v+iD$. The terminal value is zero, so integration of $-\partial_xQ\ge S(x)$ gives the second result. When $i=j$, one can first integrate on a shortened interval and pass to the diagonal endpoint using <a href="#eq:lag-Q-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Q-diagonal">[eq:lag-Q-diagonal]</a>.

Figure <a href="#fig:schur-endpoint-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:schur-endpoint-paths">1</a> displays these integrations with the half-period indices held fixed.

<figure id="fig:schur-endpoint-paths">

<figcaption>The two paths in folded endpoint coordinates $x = a + iD$, $y = b + jD$, for $0 < u < v < D$. The first starts at $Q = 0$ and increases the second endpoint; the second increases the first endpoint toward another zero. The zeros come from the diagonal when $i = j$ and from the cut-pair identity otherwise. Each moving endpoint stays in one open half-period. When $i = 1, j = 0$, the actual order is $u + D > v$, and both positive-semidefinite lower bounds still apply.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-schur-endpoint-paths"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:schur-endpoint-paths`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 11 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A 2D Cartesian diagram with horizontal axis $a$ and vertical axis $b$. Tick marks on axis $a$ are $0, u, v, D$; tick marks on axis $b$ are $0, u, v, D$. A dashed square connects $(0,0)$ to $(D,D)$, with a dashed diagonal line extending through $(0,0)$ and $(D,D)$. Dotted lines run vertically and horizontally through $u, v, D$. A solid vertical directed arrow goes from $(u, u)$ on the diagonal up to $(u, v)$, labeled $\partial_y Q \ge S(y)$ on the left; the start point $(u, u)$ is labeled $Q(u + iD, u + jD) = 0$, and the vertex $(u, v)$ is labeled $Q(u + iD, v + jD)$. From $(u, v)$, a solid horizontal directed arrow goes to $(v, v)$ on the diagonal, labeled $-\partial_x Q \ge S(x)$ above; the end point $(v, v)$ is labeled $Q(v + iD, v + jD) = 0$. Above the diagram is the coordinate formula $x = a+iD, y = b+jD, i, j \in \{0, 1\}$; below the horizontal axis is $0 < u < v < D$ (folded endpoint coordinates).
```


Both moving endpoints stay in one open half-period, so neither path passes through a chart pole. In particular, when $i=1,j=0$ the actual endpoint order is $u+D>v$. The first path starts at $(u+D,u)$ and the second ends at $(v+D,v)$; the same zero values and the same derivative inequalities apply. Thus neither matrix inequality reverses in this case. Finally, the integrals on their right sides are positive definite, so $Q(u+iD,v+jD)>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Scalar lower bounds for the two factors

For distinct $x,y\in\mathcal I$, define <a id="eq:lag-two-factors"></a>$$f_H(x,y)=
 \frac{|\det(H(y)-H(x))|}
 {\sqrt{\det H'(x)\det H'(y)}},\qquad
 f_K(x,y)=
 \frac{|\det Q(x,y)|}
 {\sqrt{\det S(x)\det S(y)}}.
 \label{eq:lag-two-factors}$$ These auxiliary factors are symmetric under exchanging the actual endpoints. Combining <a href="#eq:lag-graph-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-graph-determinant">[eq:lag-graph-determinant]</a>, <a href="#eq:lag-velocity-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-velocity-factorization">[eq:lag-velocity-factorization]</a>, and <a href="#eq:lag-Schur-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Schur-factorization">[eq:lag-Schur-factorization]</a> gives <a id="eq:lag-factorization"></a>$$f(x,y)=f_H(x,y)f_K(x,y).
 \label{eq:lag-factorization}$$ The factors are positive away from the cut pairs $|y-x|=D$; at those pairs $f_K=0$.

<a id="stmt-lag-block-bounds"></a>
<div id="lag:block-bounds" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:lag-H-scalar-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:lag-K-scalar-bound"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Scalar block bounds). *With the profiles of Proposition <a href="#lag:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:profiles">8</a>, every pair of distinct chart endpoints satisfies $$f_H(x,y)^{1/m}\ge
 \frac{\left|\int_x^y h(t)\,dt\right|}{\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}}.
 \label{eq:lag-H-scalar-bound}$$ For $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\in\{0,1\}$ one has $$f_K(u+iD,v+jD)^{1/k}\ge
 \frac{
  \left(\int_u^v s_i(t)\,dt\right)^{1/2}
  \left(\int_u^v s_j(t)\,dt\right)^{1/2}}
 {\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)}}.
 \label{eq:lag-K-scalar-bound}$$ Together with endpoint exchange, these bounds cover the full square $(0,T)^2$ except the chart, diagonal, and cut lines, a set of planar measure zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lag-block-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lag-block-bounds</summary>

<a id="eq:lag-Minkowski"></a>
*Proof.* We first record the determinant inequality used in both blocks. For a positive definite $r\times r$ matrix $M$, write $\delta_r(M)=\det(M)^{1/r}$. The elementary variational formula <a id="eq:lag-determinant-variational"></a>$$\delta_r(M)=
 \inf_{\substack{P>0\\\det P=1}}\frac1r\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PM)
 \label{eq:lag-determinant-variational}$$ follows by applying the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality to the eigenvalues of $M^{1/2}PM^{1/2}$. Equality is attained at $P=\delta_r(M)M^{-1}$. The formula proves monotonicity under the positive-semidefinite order. It also proves, for any continuous positive definite matrix function on a compact interval, $$\delta_r\left(\int_a^b M(t)\,dt\right)
 \ge\int_a^b\delta_r(M(t))\,dt.
 \label{eq:lag-Minkowski}$$ Indeed, for every admissible $P$ the integral of $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PM(t))/r$ is at least the right side, and one then takes the infimum over $P$. This is the integral form of Minkowski’s determinant inequality; it retains the degree-one homogeneity of the determinant root.

Apply <a href="#eq:lag-Minkowski" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Minkowski">[eq:lag-Minkowski]</a> to $H'$ on the interval between $x$ and $y$. Equation <a href="#eq:lag-H-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-H-difference">[eq:lag-H-difference]</a> gives $$|\det(\Delta H)|^{1/m}
 \ge\left|\int_x^y\det(H'(t))^{1/m}\,dt\right|.$$ For $y<x$ this uses the positive matrix $-\Delta H$. Division by $$\bigl(\det H'(x)\det H'(y)\bigr)^{1/(2m)}
 =\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}$$ proves <a href="#eq:lag-H-scalar-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-H-scalar-bound">[eq:lag-H-scalar-bound]</a>.

Now put $Q=Q(u+iD,v+jD)>0$. Each of the two matrix bounds in Proposition <a href="#lag:schur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:schur">9</a>, followed by determinant monotonicity and <a href="#eq:lag-Minkowski" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-Minkowski">[eq:lag-Minkowski]</a>, gives $$\delta_k(Q)\ge\int_u^v s_i(t)\,dt,
 \qquad
 \delta_k(Q)\ge\int_u^v s_j(t)\,dt.$$ Thus $\delta_k(Q)$ dominates their maximum and therefore their geometric mean. The denominator in the $k$th root of $f_K$ is exactly $$\bigl(\det S(u+iD)\det S(v+jD)\bigr)^{1/(2k)}
 =\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:lag-K-scalar-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lag-K-scalar-bound">[eq:lag-K-scalar-bound]</a> with the displayed powers.

Finally, every endpoint in $\mathcal I$ has a unique expression $u+iD$ with $0<u<D$ and $i\in\{0,1\}$. For a pair whose folded parameters differ, order those parameters as $u<v$, exchanging the actual endpoints if necessary. The preceding bounds apply for every choice of the two half-period indices. Equal folded parameters mean either a diagonal pair or a cut pair. The remaining excluded pairs have an endpoint at $D$. These are finitely many lines, and symmetry of both factors justifies the endpoint exchange everywhere else. ◻

</details>

The two determinant roots have now been compared with scalar interval integrals. Proposition <a href="#lag:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:profiles">8</a> supplies exactly their endpoint control. The next section combines these bounds by a weighted logarithmic identity.

<a id="weighted:section"></a>
### Weighted comparison along a closed geodesic

We retain $D=\pi/2$, $T=\pi$, $d=n-1$, and $m=d-k$, with $m,k\geq1$. The factorization $f=f_Hf_K$ turns the logarithm of the ratio to the model density into a sum of two block contributions. We will show that each contribution has nonnegative integral with weight $w(|y-x|)$. The scalar identity below cancels the integrated endpoint terms in the block bounds. A strict-convexity remainder will then give the determinant comparison and identify its equality case, without separately classifying equality in the matrix estimates.

The scalar identity is a logarithmic form of the weighted cancellation in Kazdan’s proof of the Berger–Kazdan inequality (Kazdan 1978, Appendix E, especially E.4–E.8) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Kazdan1978AppendixE](#ref-Kazdan1978AppendixE)]. At interval length $\pi$, writing Kazdan’s profile as $\phi(t)=\sin(t)e^{u(t)}$ gives $p=\phi^{-2}$, $p_0(t)=\sin^{-2}t$, $g=\log(p/p_0)=-2u$, and weight $\rho(t)=W(t)/\sin t$. We give the version needed here, with bounded logarithmic ratios and a measurable weight, including the endpoint integrability. Its application to the two factors depends on the folded symmetry proved below.

<a id="stmt-weighted-scalar"></a>
<div id="weighted:scalar" class="lemma">

<a id="weighted:scalar-identity"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Weighted scalar identity). *Let $b>0$, $\lambda=\pi/b$, and $$p_0(t)=\frac{\lambda^2}{\sin^2(\lambda t)},\qquad 0<t<b.$$ Let $p:(0,b)\to(0,\infty)$ be continuous and suppose that $g=\log(p/p_0)$ is bounded. Let $W$ be nonnegative and measurable on $(0,b)$, with $W(b-z)=W(z)$ almost everywhere and $W(z)/\sin(\lambda z)$ essentially bounded. Write $$\Delta_b=\{(x,y):0<x<y<b\},\qquad
 d\nu_{xy}(t)=\frac{p_0(t)\,dt}{\int_x^y p_0},\quad x<t<y.$$ Then all the following integrals are finite, and $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x)
 \left[\log\frac{\int_x^y p}{\int_x^y p_0}
       -\frac{g(x)+g(y)}2\right]dx\,dy \notag\\
 &\qquad=\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x)
 \left[\log\int_x^y e^{g(t)}\,d\nu_{xy}(t)
       -\int_x^y g(t)\,d\nu_{xy}(t)\right]dx\,dy
 \ \geq\ 0.\label{weighted:scalar-identity}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weighted-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-weighted-scalar</summary>

<a id="weighted:chord"></a>
*Proof.* Direct integration gives $$P_0(x,y):=\int_x^y p_0
 =\frac{\lambda\sin(\lambda(y-x))}
        {\sin(\lambda x)\sin(\lambda y)},\qquad
 \frac{P_0(x,y)}{\sqrt{p_0(x)p_0(y)}}
 =\frac{\sin(\lambda(y-x))}{\lambda}.
 \label{weighted:chord}$$ The measure $\nu_{xy}$ is a probability measure. Both its average of $g$ and $\log\int e^g\,d\nu_{xy}=\log(\int p/\int p_0)$ have absolute value at most $\|g\|_\infty$. Since $W$ is essentially bounded, the asserted finiteness follows already from these bounds. Jensen’s inequality makes the integrand on the second line of <a href="#weighted:scalar-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:scalar-identity">[weighted:scalar-identity]</a> nonnegative. It remains to establish the equality of the integrated linear terms.

<a id="weighted:coefficient"></a>
Set $K(z)=W(z)/\sin(\lambda z)$ and $I_W=\int_0^b W(z)\,dz$, and define $$F(t)=\int_{0<x<t<y<b}
 K(y-x)\sin(\lambda x)\sin(\lambda y)\,dx\,dy.$$ Here the two-variable kernel $q(x,y)=K(y-x)\sin(\lambda x)\sin(\lambda y)$ is essentially bounded. Fubini gives $$F(t)=\int_0^t\left(\int_x^b q(x,y)\,dy\right)dx
       -\int_0^t\left(\int_0^y q(x,y)\,dx\right)dy.$$ Thus $F$ is absolutely continuous on $[0,b]$, $F(0)=0$, and, for almost every $t$, $$\begin{aligned}
 F'(t)
 &=\sin(\lambda t)\left[
   \int_0^{b-t}K(z)\sin(\lambda(t+z))\,dz
   -\int_0^t K(z)\sin(\lambda(t-z))\,dz\right]\\
 &=\sin(\lambda t)\int_0^b K(z)\sin(\lambda(t+z))\,dz\\
 &=I_W\sin(\lambda t)\cos(\lambda t).
\end{aligned}$$ For the second equality substitute $b-z$ in the subtracted integral: $K(b-z)=K(z)$ and $\sin(\lambda(t-b+z))=-\sin(\lambda(t+z))$. For the last equality, symmetry gives $\int_0^b K(z)\cos(\lambda z)\,dz=0$, while $\int_0^b K(z)\sin(\lambda z)\,dz=I_W$. Integration therefore yields $$F(t)=\frac{I_W}{2\lambda}\sin^2(\lambda t),\qquad
 \frac{p_0(t)}{\lambda}F(t)=\frac{I_W}{2}.
 \label{weighted:coefficient}$$ This argument has not differentiated the measurable weight.

Using <a href="#weighted:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:chord">[weighted:chord]</a> and then <a href="#weighted:coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:coefficient">[weighted:coefficient]</a>, the integrated interval averages are $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x)\int_x^y g(t)\,d\nu_{xy}(t)\,dx\,dy
 &=\int_0^b g(t)\frac{p_0(t)}{\lambda}F(t)\,dt\\
 &=\frac{I_W}{2}\int_0^b g(t)\,dt.
\end{aligned}$$ The exchange is absolutely integrable: replacing $g$ by $|g|$ in the left side bounds it by $\|g\|_\infty\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x)\,dx\,dy$. In particular, no integral of $p_0$ over the entire interval is taken. The endpoint terms have exactly the same value, since $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac12\int_{\Delta_b}W(y-x)(g(x)+g(y))\,dx\,dy\\
 &\qquad=\frac12\int_0^b g(t)
 \left[\int_0^{b-t}W(z)\,dz+\int_0^t W(z)\,dz\right]dt
 =\frac{I_W}{2}\int_0^b g(t)\,dt.
\end{aligned}$$ The final equality again uses $W(b-z)=W(z)$. Subtracting these equal finite quantities proves the identity and hence the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-weighted-geodesic"></a>
<div id="weighted:geodesic" class="theorem">

<a id="weighted:comparison"></a>
**Theorem 12** (Comparison on each geodesic). *In the normalized intermediate case of Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a>, let $f(x,y)$ be the Jacobi determinant of any fixed unit-speed geodesic, as in Proposition <a href="#lag:determinant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:determinant">6</a>. Put $$\mathcal R=(0,T)^2,\qquad
 w(z)=(\sin z)^m\left|\frac{\sin(2z)}2\right|^k
 \quad(0\leq z\leq T).$$ Then $$\int_{\mathcal R}f(x,y)\,dx\,dy
 \ \geq\ \int_{\mathcal R}w(|y-x|)\,dx\,dy.
 \label{weighted:comparison}$$ Equality holds if and only if $f(x,y)=w(|y-x|)$ for every $(x,y)\in[0,T]^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weighted-geodesic"><summary>Proof of stmt-weighted-geodesic</summary>

*Proof.* All constants in this proof may depend on the chosen geodesic. Write $w_{xy}=w(|y-x|)$. We first work off the null set $$E=\{(x,y)\in\mathcal R:
       x=D\text{ or }y=D\text{ or }x=y\text{ or }|y-x|=D\}.$$ There $f,w_{xy}$, and the two determinant factors $f_H,f_K$ of Proposition <a href="#lag:block-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:block-bounds">10</a> are positive, and $f=f_Hf_K$. Define $$L_H(x,y)=\log\frac{f_H(x,y)}{\sin^m(|y-x|)},\qquad
 L_K(x,y)=\log\frac{f_K(x,y)}{|\sin(2|y-x|)/2|^k}.$$ Set their values on $E$ equal to zero for integration. Both are symmetric in the actual endpoint slots $x,y$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Finiteness of the logarithmic integrals.

<a id="weighted:log-lower-bounds"></a>
The profile bounds in Proposition <a href="#lag:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:profiles">8</a> give constants $0<\alpha\leq\beta<\infty$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \alpha&\leq\frac{h(t)}{\csc^2t}\leq\beta
 &&(0<t<T),\\
 \alpha&\leq\frac{s_i(t)}{4\csc^2(2t)}\leq\beta
 &&(0<t<D,\ i=0,1).
\end{aligned}$$ Set $\eta=\alpha/\beta\leq1$. For $x<y$, the first bound in Proposition <a href="#lag:block-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:block-bounds">10</a> and the chord identity <a href="#weighted:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:chord">[weighted:chord]</a> imply $$f_H(x,y)^{1/m}
 \geq\frac{\int_x^y h}{\sqrt{h(x)h(y)}}
 \geq\eta\sin(y-x).$$ Symmetry gives this lower bound for either endpoint order. Similarly, put $B_0(z)=\sin(2z)/2$ for $0<z<D$. For $0<u<v<D$ and $i,j\in\{0,1\}$, the second block bound gives $$f_K(u+iD,v+jD)^{1/k}
 \geq\frac{(\int_u^v s_i)^{1/2}(\int_u^v s_j)^{1/2}}
              {\sqrt{s_i(u)s_j(v)}}
 \geq\eta B_0(v-u).$$ In these coordinates $B_0(v-u)=|\sin(2|y-x|)/2|$. Every pair outside $E$ has this form, possibly after exchanging endpoints. Consequently $$L_H\geq m\log\eta,
 \qquad L_K\geq k\log\eta,
 \qquad L_H+L_K=L:=\log(f/w_{xy}).
 \label{weighted:log-lower-bounds}$$ With $a^+=\max(a,0)$, the elementary estimate $$w_{xy}L^+\leq f$$ and continuity of $f$ on the compact square prove integrability of $w_{xy}L^+$. The individual negative parts are bounded by <a href="#weighted:log-lower-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:log-lower-bounds">[weighted:log-lower-bounds]</a>; also $$L_H^+\leq L^+-k\log\eta,\qquad
 L_K^+\leq L^+-m\log\eta.$$ Thus $w_{xy}L_H$, $w_{xy}L_K$, and $w_{xy}L$ are all absolutely integrable. The following additions of logarithmic integrals therefore involve only finite quantities.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### The $H$ factor on the full square.

<a id="weighted:H-log"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#weighted:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:scalar">11</a> with $b=T$, $\lambda=1$, $p=h$, and $W=w$. Its assumptions hold by Proposition <a href="#lag:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:profiles">8</a> and $$w(T-z)=w(z),\qquad
 \frac{w(z)}{\sin z}
 =\sin^{m-1}z\left|\frac{\sin(2z)}2\right|^k,$$ which is bounded because $m\geq1$. Writing $g_H=\log(h/\csc^2t)$, the first block bound and <a href="#weighted:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:chord">[weighted:chord]</a> give, for $0<x<y<T$ outside $E$, $$\frac{L_H(x,y)}m
 \geq\log\frac{\int_x^y h(t)\,dt}{\int_x^y\csc^2t\,dt}
       -\frac{g_H(x)+g_H(y)}2.$$ After multiplication by $w(y-x)$, Lemma <a href="#weighted:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:scalar">11</a> makes the integral of the right side nonnegative. Multiplication by $m$ and endpoint symmetry then yield $$\int_{\mathcal R}w_{xy}L_H(x,y)\,dx\,dy\geq0.
 \label{weighted:H-log}$$

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### The $K$ factor and the four folded weights.

<a id="weighted:folded-bound"></a>
Put $p_0(t)=4\csc^2(2t)$ on $(0,D)$ and define $$g_i(t)=\log\frac{s_i(t)}{p_0(t)},\qquad
 A_i(u,v)=\log\frac{\int_u^v s_i(t)\,dt}{\int_u^v p_0(t)\,dt}
 \quad(i=0,1).$$ For $(u,v)\in\Delta_D$, $z=v-u$, $x=u+iD$, and $y=v+jD$, the second block bound gives the precise logarithmic inequality $$\frac{L_K(u+iD,v+jD)}k
 \geq\frac12\bigl[A_i(u,v)+A_j(u,v)-g_i(u)-g_j(v)\bigr].
 \label{weighted:folded-bound}$$ Indeed, <a href="#weighted:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:chord">[weighted:chord]</a> with $\lambda=2$ converts the reference interval integral and its endpoint factors into $B_0(z)$. The actual separation is $z$ if $i=j$, $D+z$ if $(i,j)=(0,1)$, and $D-z$ if $(i,j)=(1,0)$. In the last case $x>y$; the bound in Proposition <a href="#lag:block-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:block-bounds">10</a> includes this order. All three separations have the same absolute fast model factor $B_0(z)$.

The four weights $w_{ij}(z)=w(|z+(j-i)D|)$ form the matrix <a id="weighted:weight-matrix"></a>$$(w_{ij}(z))_{i,j=0}^1
 =B_0(z)^k
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \sin^m z&\cos^m z\\
  \cos^m z&\sin^m z
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \label{weighted:weight-matrix}$$ Every row and every column sums to $$W_D(z)=(\sin^m z+\cos^m z)B_0(z)^k.$$ This is symmetric under $z\mapsto D-z$, and $$\frac{W_D(z)}{\sin(2z)}
 =2^{-k}(\sin^m z+\cos^m z)\sin^{k-1}(2z)$$ is bounded for every $k\geq1$. When $k=1$, its limit at either endpoint is $1/2$, so this case also satisfies Lemma <a href="#weighted:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:scalar">11</a>.

Multiply <a href="#weighted:folded-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:folded-bound">[weighted:folded-bound]</a> by $w_{ij}(z)$ and sum over $i,j$. The row sums control the terms indexed by $i$, and the column sums control those indexed by $j$, giving exactly <a id="weighted:folded-sum"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i,j=0}^1 w_{ij}(z)\frac{L_K(u+iD,v+jD)}k
 &\geq W_D(z)\sum_{i=0}^1
 \left[A_i(u,v)-\frac{g_i(u)+g_i(v)}2\right].
 \label{weighted:folded-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Lemma <a href="#weighted:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:scalar">11</a>, applied on $(0,D)$ separately to $s_0$ and $s_1$ with weight $W_D$, shows that the right side has nonnegative integral over $\Delta_D$.

<a id="weighted:K-log"></a>
For completeness, the four images of $\Delta_D$ used above consist precisely of pairs whose first endpoint has smaller residue modulo $D$ than the second. Their transposes contain precisely the opposite residue order. These eight disjoint regions cover $\mathcal R\setminus E$; each coordinate change has Jacobian one. Since $w_{xy}L_K(x,y)$ is symmetric, it follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_{\mathcal R}w_{xy}L_K(x,y)\,dx\,dy\notag\\
 &\qquad=2k\int_{\Delta_D}\sum_{i,j=0}^1
 w_{ij}(v-u)\frac{L_K(u+iD,v+jD)}k\,du\,dv
 \ \geq\ 0.
 \label{weighted:K-log}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the factor $2$ accounts for endpoint exchange and the factor $k$ restores the logarithm of $f_K$ from that of its $k$th root.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Comparison and equality.

Adding <a href="#weighted:H-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:H-log">[weighted:H-log]</a> and <a href="#weighted:K-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:K-log">[weighted:K-log]</a> gives $$\int_{\mathcal R}w_{xy}\log(f/w_{xy})\,dx\,dy\geq0.$$ For $r>0$, the strictly convex function $$\Phi(r)=r-1-\log r$$ is nonnegative and vanishes exactly at $r=1$. On $\mathcal R\setminus E$ put $r=f/w_{xy}$. All terms in the identity <a id="weighted:strict-gap"></a>$$\int_{\mathcal R}(f-w_{xy})\,dx\,dy
 =\int_{\mathcal R}w_{xy}\log r\,dx\,dy
  +\int_{\mathcal R}w_{xy}\Phi(r)\,dx\,dy
 \label{weighted:strict-gap}$$ are finite by the preceding integrability argument. Both terms on the right are nonnegative, proving <a href="#weighted:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:comparison">[weighted:comparison]</a>. If equality holds, the last integral is zero. Since $w_{xy}>0$ off the diagonal and cut lines, $\Phi(r)=0$ almost everywhere, hence $f=w_{xy}$ almost everywhere in $\mathcal R$. Continuity of both functions on $[0,T]^2$ extends this identity to every pair, including the exceptional lines and boundary. Conversely, that pointwise identity gives equality in <a href="#weighted:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:comparison">[weighted:comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="rig:section"></a>
### Global comparison and the equality case

We retain all intermediate model types and the normalization of Section <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">2</a>. The preceding geodesic comparison now gives the global volume bound. Its nonnegative defect will also retain enough information to identify the equality metric.

For $\xi\in U$, write $\gamma_\xi$ for the geodesic with initial velocity $\xi$ and $f_\xi$ for the determinant of Proposition <a href="#lag:determinant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lag:determinant">6</a>. Put $$J(\xi,t)=f_\xi(0,t).$$ This is the absolute determinant of the normal Jacobi tensor with initial value zero and derivative $\mathrm{Id}$. It is continuous in $(\xi,t)$, positive for $0<t<D$, and agrees there with the usual polar volume density.

<a id="stmt-rig-polar"></a>
<div id="rig:polar" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Polar integration over a full period). *For every $p\in M$, $$\int_{S_pM}\int_0^D J(\xi,t)\,dt\,d\sigma_p(\xi)=V,
 \qquad
 \int_{S_pM}\int_0^T J(\xi,t)\,dt\,d\sigma_p(\xi)=2V.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-polar"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-polar</summary>

*Proof.* The open injectivity ball maps diffeomorphically onto the complement of the cut locus. The latter is a smooth submanifold of dimension $d-k<n$, hence has zero Riemannian volume. Polar coordinates therefore prove the first formula. Periodicity of the Jacobi data and reversal of the geodesic give $$J(\xi,T-t)=J(\xi,-t)=J(-\xi,t).$$ The sign in the reversed initial derivative disappears under the absolute determinant. The substitution $t\mapsto T-t$ in the second half-period, followed by the area-preserving antipodal map on $S_pM$, proves the second formula. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-polar-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-polar</summary>

<a id="rig:geodesic-defect"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:comparison">2</a>.* The circle, sphere-type, and real-projective-type cases are already covered in Section <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">2</a>. In an intermediate case use the normalization <a href="#geom:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:normalization">[geom:normalization]</a>. Theorem <a href="#weighted:geodesic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:geodesic">12</a> says that $$\label{rig:geodesic-defect}
 \delta(\xi):=\int_0^T\int_0^T
   \bigl(f_\xi(x,y)-w(|y-x|)\bigr)\,dx\,dy\ge0.$$ Time translation along a geodesic gives $$f_\xi(x,y)=J\bigl(\phi_x\xi,(y-x)\bmod T\bigr).$$ The possible choice of endpoint for the residue modulo $T$ has no effect on any integral. Fubini’s theorem, translation on the time circle, flow invariance of $\mu$, and Lemma <a href="#rig:polar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rig:polar">13</a> yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_U\int_0^T\int_0^T f_\xi(x,y)\,dx\,dy\,d\mu(\xi)
 &=T\int_U\int_0^T J(\xi,t)\,dt\,d\mu(\xi)\\
 &=2TV^2.
\end{aligned}$$ All integrands are continuous and bounded on the compact domains in question. Since $w(T-t)=w(t)$, its periodic extension gives likewise $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_U\int_0^T\int_0^T w(|y-x|)\,dx\,dy\,d\mu(\xi)
 &=\sigma_d V T\int_0^T w(t)\,dt\\
 &=2TVV_0.
\end{aligned}$$ Integrating <a href="#rig:geodesic-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:geodesic-defect">[rig:geodesic-defect]</a> and dividing by $2TV>0$ proves $V\ge V_0$.

<a id="rig:radial-density"></a>
If $V=V_0$, the nonnegative function $\delta$ has integral zero. It is continuous, because the underlying Jacobi tensors depend smoothly on the initial velocity and taking the absolute determinant preserves continuity. Full support of $\mu$ forces $\delta(\xi)=0$ for every $\xi$. The equality assertion in Theorem <a href="#weighted:geodesic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:geodesic">12</a> now gives $$\label{rig:radial-density}
 J(\xi,t)=w(t),\qquad \xi\in U,\quad 0<t<D.$$

In Cartesian normal coordinates at any point, the volume density is $J(\xi,t)/t^d$. By <a href="#rig:radial-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:radial-density">[rig:radial-density]</a> it depends only on $t$. At the origin it extends smoothly with value one, as also follows directly from the normal-coordinate volume form. Thus $M$ is harmonic. It is compact and simply connected by Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a>. The compact simply connected case of the Lichnerowicz conjecture, proved by Szabó (1990) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Szabo1990](#ref-Szabo1990)], identifies its metric with a compact rank-one symmetric metric up to scale. Its type identifies the model, and the fixed diameter identifies the scale. For the radial-density definition and the concluding metric classification, see also the author’s original preprint (Szabó 1988, 2–4 and Lemmas 4.9–4.10) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Szabo1988](#ref-Szabo1988)].

Conversely, a model is isometric to itself and has volume $V_0$. Finally both sides of the volume comparison scale by the same factor under a constant metric rescaling. Undoing <a href="#geom:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:normalization">[geom:normalization]</a> proves the assertion at every diameter. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-polar-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-polar</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#thm:volume-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:volume-reduction">3</a>.* The upper bound and Theorem <a href="#thm:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:comparison">2</a> give equal volumes, so its equality case applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="inc:section"></a>
### Incidence and the quaternionic Pontryagin class

The lower-volume comparison and its metric equality case are now proved. To obtain the quaternionic upper bound, we first determine the characteristic class of an actual bundle supplied by the cut geometry: the normal four-plane bundle of cut incidence. The later loop-energy construction will use this same bundle, not only its sphere-fibration homotopy type. We use the standard integral bundle and cohomology tools in Hatcher (2002) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002)] and J. W. Milnor and Stasheff (1974); the geometric constructions and the relations needed here are proved below. In this section and the next three, suppose that $M$ has quaternionic type, so $n=4r$, $r\ge2$, and $k=3$. Choose an integral generator $u\in H^4(M;\mathbb Z)$ and an orientation with $\int_Mu^r=1$. Write $C_p=\operatorname{Cut}(p)$. The cell attachment in Section <a href="#geom:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:section">2</a> gives $$H^*(C_p;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z[u|_{C_p}]/((u|_{C_p})^r).$$ Indeed restriction is an isomorphism in every degree below $4r-1$, and $C_p$ has dimension $4r-4$. In particular it is orientable, and its fundamental class is dual to $\pm u$ in $M$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The actual normal bundle

<a id="stmt-inc-normal"></a>
<div id="inc:normal" class="proposition">

<a id="inc:normal-classes"></a>
**Proposition 14**. *The cut incidence $$F=\{(p,q)\in M\times M:q\in C_p\}$$ is a smooth embedded submanifold. Both projections are smooth fiber bundles with fiber a cut locus. If $u_1,u_2$ are the two pullbacks of $u$, its rank-four normal bundle $\mathcal V$ can be oriented so that $$\label{inc:normal-classes}
 e(\mathcal V)=u_1-u_2,\qquad p_1(\mathcal V)=2s(u_1+u_2),\qquad s\in2\mathbb Z+1.$$ Projection of $\mathcal V_{(p,q)}$ into $T_pM$ identifies it with $N_pC_q$. The unit sphere of this four-plane is exactly the great sphere of initial directions of minimizing geodesics from $p$ to $q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-normal"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-normal</summary>

*Proof.* The full cut map $$c:SM\longrightarrow M\times M,\qquad
 c(\xi)=(\pi(\xi),\exp_{\pi(\xi)}(D\xi))$$ has constant rank $4r+(4r-4)=8r-4$. Its fibers are the connected great three-spheres from Proposition <a href="#geom:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:structure">5</a>. Here constant rank and compactness give an embedded image, as follows. Constant-rank charts give local image germs through any image point. Charts overlapping along its fiber have the same germ, so connectedness of that fiber makes all these germs agree. Cover the compact fiber by finitely many such charts. Properness excludes additional inverse images outside their union over a sufficiently small neighborhood of the image point. Thus the whole image in that neighborhood is the one smooth submanifold germ. This proves the assertion. The first projection is a submersion because its composition with $c$ is $\pi$; symmetry gives the same conclusion for the second. Proper submersions are smooth fiber bundles.

<a id="inc:disjoint-planes"></a>
For $(a,b)\in \mathcal V_{(p,q)}\subset T_pM\oplus T_qM$, orthogonality to $(T_pC_q,0)$ gives $a\in N_pC_q$. If $a=0$, surjectivity of the second projection on $T_{(p,q)}F$ forces $b=0$. The projection to $N_pC_q$ is therefore injective and, both spaces having dimension four, an isomorphism. First variation for the constant distance $D$ shows that the initial directions from $p$ to $q$ are normal to $C_q$. Their great sphere has the full dimension of $S(N_pC_q)$, proving the last assertion. In particular, if $q_1,q_2\in C_p$ are distinct, then $$\label{inc:disjoint-planes}
 N_pC_{q_1}\cap N_pC_{q_2}=\{0\}:$$ a common nonzero direction would reach both points at time $D$.

The cut loci and $M$ are simply connected, so the fibration makes $F$ simply connected. Its normal bundle is orientable. Leray–Hirsch (Hatcher 2002, Theorem 4D.1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002)], using $1,u_2,\ldots,u_2^{r-1}$ on the fibers, and disjointness from the diagonal give the integral presentation <a id="inc:ring"></a>$$\label{inc:ring}
 H^*(F;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z[u_1,u_2]/(u_1^{r+1},\Delta(u_1,u_2)),
 \qquad \Delta(a,b)=\sum_{j=0}^r a^jb^{r-j}.$$ To justify completeness, the displayed relation is the restriction of the diagonal class in $M\times M$. It is monic of degree $r$ in $u_2$, so the proposed quotient has exactly the Leray–Hirsch basis. The dual of $F$ in $M\times M$ restricts on either factor slice to $\pm u$. Its restriction to the disjoint diagonal is zero. Its two factor coefficients are consequently opposite, and after an orientation choice its restriction to $F$ is $u_1-u_2$. The self-intersection formula gives the asserted Euler class.

The interchange of factors preserves the unoriented normal bundle. Since $u_1,u_2$ are an integral basis of $H^4(F;\mathbb Z)$, we have $p_1(\mathcal V)=t(u_1+u_2)$ for an integer $t$. Also $H^2(F;\mathbb Z/2)=0$, so $\mathcal V$ is spin. We use the classical identification $\operatorname{Spin}(4)=\operatorname{SU}(2)\times\operatorname{SU}(2)$ and its two complex two-dimensional half-spin representations; see Moore (2010, secs. 2.1–2.2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Moore2010](#ref-Moore2010)]. For an oriented spin four-plane bundle, its two complex half-spin bundles have second Chern classes $$\frac{-p_1(\mathcal V)+2e(\mathcal V)}4,\qquad
 \frac{-p_1(\mathcal V)-2e(\mathcal V)}4.$$ This follows directly from the formal half-spin weights $\pm(t_1+t_2)/2$ and $\pm(t_1-t_2)/2$, where $p_1=t_1^2+t_2^2$ and $e=t_1t_2$. Integrality in the basis $u_1,u_2$ implies $t\equiv2\pmod4$. Writing $t=2s$ proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

Changing $u$ to $-u$ replaces $s$ by $-s$. Reverse the orientation of $\mathcal V$ at the same time to retain $e(\mathcal V)=u_1-u_2$, and reverse the orientation of $M$ exactly when $r$ is odd to retain $\int_Mu^r=1$. We may therefore take $s\ge1$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The rank constraint from two direction planes

<a id="inc:triple-ring"></a>
For distinct $q_1,q_2\in C_p$, <a href="#inc:disjoint-planes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:disjoint-planes">[inc:disjoint-planes]</a> places two four-planes with zero intersection inside $T_pM$. Their direct sum has a complement of rank $4(r-2)$, which limits the degree of its total Pontryagin class. We will use this rank restriction to determine $p(TM)$ from $s$. The parameter space must therefore exclude coincident cut points; put $$Z=\{(p,q_1,q_2):q_1,q_2\in C_p,\ q_1\ne q_2\}.$$ Use $v,x_1,x_2$ for the pullbacks of $u$ from its three points. In this section polynomial degree is one quarter of cohomological degree. In degrees divisible by four the rational cohomology of $Z$ is the algebra $$\label{inc:triple-ring}
 A=\mathbb Q[v,x_1,x_2]/(v^{r+1},\Delta(v,x_1),S(v,x_1,x_2)),
 \quad
 S(v,x_1,x_2)=\sum_{i+j+\ell=r-1}v^ix_1^jx_2^\ell.$$ Here and below this assertion identifies the subalgebra consisting of these degrees; no claim about other degrees is needed.

<a id="inc:free-basis"></a>
For completeness, before removing $q_1=q_2$, Leray–Hirsch gives the relations $v^{r+1}$, $\Delta(v,x_1)$ and $\Delta(v,x_2)$. The relative diagonal has codimension $4(r-1)$. Its dual is $\pm S$. To see this, the dual is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $r-1$ annihilated by $x_1-x_2$. In degree $r$ the only relations are the two displayed $\Delta$ polynomials; their difference is $(x_1-x_2)S$. More explicitly, multiplying a representative of the dual by $x_1-x_2$ gives a constant linear combination of those two relations. Setting $x_1=x_2$ makes the two constants opposite. Cancellation of $x_1-x_2$ then takes place in the ordinary polynomial ring, before passage to cohomology, and shows that the dual is proportional to $S$. Restriction to a fixed-$p$ fiber gives the ordinary diagonal class of $C_p\times C_p$, fixing the factor as $\pm1$. The Thom sequence for removing the relative diagonal is surjective onto the complement in degrees divisible by four, because the relevant next cohomology group of $F$ is zero. Restriction from the ambient fiber product to that diagonal is surjective, so the projection formula identifies the kernel with the ideal of its dual. The relation $\Delta(v,x_2)$ then becomes redundant, proving <a href="#inc:triple-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:triple-ring">[inc:triple-ring]</a>. Successive division by its two monic relations proves that $A$ is free over $\mathbb Q[v]/(v^{r+1})$, with basis $$\label{inc:free-basis}
 \{x_1^a x_2^b:0\le a<r,\ 0\le b<r-1\}.$$

<a id="stmt-inc-pontryagin"></a>
<div id="inc:pontryagin" class="theorem">

**Theorem 15**. *The total Pontryagin class of $M$ is <a id="inc:pontryagin-formula"></a>$$\label{inc:pontryagin-formula}
 p(TM)=\frac{(1+2su+u^2)^{r+1}}{1+4su}
 \quad\text{in }H^*(M;\mathbb Q).$$ The quotient means its power-series expansion truncated by $u^{r+1}=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-pontryagin"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-pontryagin</summary>

<a id="inc:complement"></a>
*Proof.* Over $Z$, the two actual normal four-planes in <a href="#inc:disjoint-planes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:disjoint-planes">[inc:disjoint-planes]</a> have direct sum inside the pullback of $TM$ from $p$. Choose a complementary bundle $W$ of rank $4(r-2)$. Set $$D_s(v,x)=1+2s(v+x)+(v-x)^2,
 \qquad p_0(v)=\frac{(1+2sv+v^2)^{r+1}}{1+4sv}.$$ The rank-four identity $p_2=e^2$ (J. W. Milnor and Stasheff 1974, Corollary 15.8) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[MilnorStasheff1974](#ref-MilnorStasheff1974)] and Proposition <a href="#inc:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:normal">14</a> give $$\label{inc:complement}
 p(W)=\frac{p(TM)(v)}{D_s(v,x_1)D_s(v,x_2)}.$$ Its polynomial degree is at most $t_0=2r-4$.

We will prove that the formal characteristic class $$\mathcal W_0=\frac{p_0(v)}{D_s(v,x_1)D_s(v,x_2)}\in A$$ has degree at most $t_0$ and a degree-$t_0$ component that is nonzero modulo $v$. The freeness in <a href="#inc:free-basis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:free-basis">[inc:free-basis]</a> will then show that any first discrepancy between $p(TM)(v)$ and $p_0(v)$ violates the rank bound on $W$. For the degree bound we use only the algebra <a href="#inc:triple-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:triple-ring">[inc:triple-ring]</a>, without presuming the answer for $TM$. Successive monic polynomial divisions factor $$T^{r+1}=(T-v)(T-x_1)(T-x_2)
                   \prod_{j=4}^{r+1}(T-z_j)$$ in an extension of $A$ obtained by adjoining roots of monic polynomials. Give each root degree one; the monic relations are homogeneous for this grading. Each adjoining algebra is free with a basis containing $1$, so this extension is injective. The positive-degree elementary symmetric functions of all $r+1$ roots vanish. Consequently, the product of $D_s(v,z)$ over all these roots is $(1+2sv+v^2)^{r+1}$. The factor at $z=v$ is $1+4sv$. Dividing these constant-one series therefore gives $$\mathcal W_0=\prod_{j=4}^{r+1}D_s(v,z_j).$$ There are $r-2$ factors, each of degree at most two, as required.

<a id="inc:reciprocal-identity"></a>
We also need the top component $(\mathcal W_0)_{t_0}$ to be nonzero modulo $v$. For $r=2$ it is $1$. For $r\ge3$, let $$(1+2sx+x^2)^{-1}=\sum_{j\ge0}a_jx^j.$$ The recursion $a_0=1$, $a_1=-2s$ and $a_j=-2sa_{j-1}-a_{j-2}$ gives $$\label{inc:reciprocal-identity}
 a_j^2-a_{j-1}a_{j+1}=1\qquad(j\ge1).$$ Indeed the left side is unchanged on replacing $j$ by $j-1$, and its initial value is one. Modulo $v$ the algebra can also be presented as $$\mathbb Q[x_1,x_2]/
 \left(x_1^r,x_2^r,\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}x_1^jx_2^{r-1-j}\right).$$ In degree $2r-4$ the surviving monomials after the first two truncations are $$x_1^{r-3}x_2^{r-1},\qquad
 x_1^{r-2}x_2^{r-2},\qquad x_1^{r-1}x_2^{r-3}.$$ The remaining ideal gives exactly their sum as a relation: multiplying its generator by any monomial of degree $r-3$ gives this same sum. Their coefficients in $\mathcal W_0$ are, in order, $a_{r-3}a_{r-1},a_{r-2}^2,a_{r-3}a_{r-1}$. Equation <a href="#inc:reciprocal-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:reciprocal-identity">[inc:reciprocal-identity]</a> makes the middle coefficient differ from either outside coefficient by one. Thus the component modulo $v$ is the nonzero class of $x_1^{r-2}x_2^{r-2}$, one of the monomials in <a href="#inc:free-basis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:free-basis">[inc:free-basis]</a>.

Finally suppose that $p(TM)(v)/p_0(v)$ first differs from $1$ in degree $i$, where $1\le i\le r$. Write its first coefficient as $b\ne0$. In degree $t_0+i$, modulo $v^{i+1}$, <a href="#inc:complement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:complement">[inc:complement]</a> is $b v^i(\mathcal W_0)_{t_0}$. This is nonzero by <a href="#inc:free-basis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:free-basis">[inc:free-basis]</a> and the preceding calculation: in a free module over $\mathbb Q[v]/(v^{r+1})$, a coefficient nonzero modulo $v$ remains nonzero after multiplication by $v^i$, modulo $v^{i+1}$, for $i\le r$. It contradicts the rank bound on $W$. Therefore there is no such $i$, which proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sig:section"></a>
### The signature determines the incidence coefficient

The incidence construction has reduced the bundle calculation to one odd integer $s$. The signature theorem determines it in every quaternionic rank. The small ranks follow from exact coefficients; a single contour estimate treats all larger ranks.

<a id="stmt-sig-coefficient"></a>
<div id="sig:coefficient" class="theorem">

**Theorem 16**. *In Proposition <a href="#inc:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:normal">14</a>, $|s|=1$. In particular, the actual tautological four-plane bundle of every tangent great-sphere fibration at a point of $M$ satisfies $p_1=\pm2e$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sig-coefficient"><summary>Proof of stmt-sig-coefficient</summary>

<a id="sig:formula"></a>
*Proof.* Keep the choices $s\ge1$ and $\int_Mu^r=1$. Set $$a_+a_-=1,\qquad a_+^2+a_-^2=2s,\qquad
 a_+\ge a_->0,\qquad c=2\sqrt{s},
 \quad f(y)=\frac{\sqrt y}{\tanh\sqrt y}.$$ Hirzebruch’s signature theorem (J. W. Milnor and Stasheff 1974, Signature Theorem 19.4) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[MilnorStasheff1974](#ref-MilnorStasheff1974)], applied to Theorem <a href="#inc:pontryagin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:pontryagin">15</a> gives $$\label{sig:formula}
 \sigma_r=[u^r]
 \frac{(f(a_+^2u)f(a_-^2u))^{r+1}}{f(c^2u)}.$$ Here $f$ is its power series at zero; the two factors reflect $1+2su+u^2=(1+a_+^2u)(1+a_-^2u)$. The integral cohomology ring of $M$ gives $$\sigma_r=\begin{cases}1,&r\text{ even},\\0,&r\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}$$ Indeed for even $r$ the middle pairing has the positive basis $u^{r/2}$, and for odd $r$ its middle cohomology is zero. Expanding $f(y)=1+y/3-y^2/45+2y^3/945+\cdots$ in <a href="#sig:formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:formula">[sig:formula]</a> gives $$\sigma_2-1=\frac{8(s^2-1)}{15},\qquad
 \sigma_3=-\frac{32s(s^2-1)}{105}.$$ These prove the assertion for $r=2,3$.

<a id="sig:residue"></a>
We give uniform estimates for all $r\ge4$. Since $s$ is odd, the contrary case is $s\ge3$. We express the signature as a residue and show that the two nearest nonzero poles give a main term that dominates the remaining contour integral, forcing $(-1)^r\sigma_r>1$. Put $$\tau=\frac{a_-}{a_+}=s-\sqrt{s^2-1},\qquad
 b=\frac c{a_+}=\sqrt{2(1+\tau^2)},\qquad
 Q(z)=\tan z\tan(\tau z),\qquad \rho=\frac\pi{2b}.$$ Then $0<\tau\le3-\sqrt8<0.172$ and $\sqrt2<b<1.435$. Substitute $u=-t^2$ in <a href="#sig:formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:formula">[sig:formula]</a>. Since $f(-a^2t^2)=at/\tan(at)$ and $a_+a_-=1$, taking the coefficient by a residue and then putting $z=a_+t$ gives $$\label{sig:residue}
 (-1)^r\sigma_r=
 \frac\tau b\operatorname*{Res}_{z=0}
 \frac{\tan(bz)}{Q(z)^{r+1}}\,dz.$$ The change of variables contributes the factor $1/(ca_+)=\tau/b$; this fixes the normalization of the residue.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Contour representation.

<a id="sig:main-term"></a>
<a id="sig:horizontal"></a>
Take the rectangle with vertical sides $\Re z=\pm X$, $X=3\pi/4$, and horizontal sides $\Im z=\pm4$. Apart from zero, the only poles of the integrand in this rectangle are at $z=\pm\rho$. Zeros of $\tan z$ occur only at multiples of $\pi$, zeros of $\tan(\tau z)$ only at multiples of $\pi/\tau$, and the next poles of $\tan(bz)$ are outside because $3\pi/(2b)>X$. The poles of $\tan z$ at $\pm\pi/2$ give zeros of the reciprocal denominator, not poles. Both residues at $\pm\rho$ equal $-b^{-1}Q(\rho)^{-r-1}$, with $Q(\rho)>0$. The residue theorem therefore expresses <a href="#sig:residue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:residue">[sig:residue]</a> as $$\label{sig:main-term}
 (-1)^r\sigma_r=L+\mathcal E,\qquad
 L=\frac{2\tau}{b^2}Q(\rho)^{-r-1}.$$ Symmetry of the four sides and the triangle inequality bound $|\mathcal E|/L$ by the sum of <a id="sig:vertical"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 E_v&=\frac b\pi\int_0^4
  |\tan(b(X+iy))|
  \left(\frac{Q(\rho)}{|Q(X+iy)|}\right)^{r+1}\,dy,
  \label{sig:vertical}\\
 E_h&=\frac b\pi\int_0^X
  |\tan(b(x+4i))|
  \left(\frac{Q(\rho)}{|Q(x+4i)|}\right)^{r+1}\,dx.
  \label{sig:horizontal}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Uniform bounds.

<a id="sig:ratio"></a>
The same contour works for every $r\ge4$ once both boundary ratios are bounded by constants below one: their fifth powers then control every exponent $r+1\ge5$. We retain the $\tau$ dependence in the numerator and denominator so these bounds remain uniform as $\tau\downarrow0$. All finite decimals below denote the exact indicated rational numbers. Using $3.14<\pi<3.142$ gives $$\rho<1.111,\qquad \tan\rho<2.022,\qquad
 \tau\rho<0.192,\qquad Q(\rho)<2.30\tau.$$ For the last inequality, the increase of $\tan t/t$ and the Taylor bounds on $[0,0.192]$ give $\tan t/t<1.013$, and $2.022\cdot1.111\cdot1.013<2.30$. The same monotonicity gives $$\label{sig:ratio}
 \frac{Q(\rho)}{\tan(\tau X)}
 \le\frac{\rho\tan\rho}{X}<0.955.$$ On the vertical sides, $|\tan(X+iy)|=1$. Also $\cos(2bX)\ge0$, since $2bX=3b\pi/2\in(2\pi,5\pi/2)$, and hence $|\tan(b(X+iy))|\le1$. The elementary complex tangent identity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{|\tan(\tau(X+iy))|^2}{\tan^2(\tau X)}
 &=\frac{1+\sinh^2(\tau y)/\sin^2(\tau X)}
         {1+\sinh^2(\tau y)/\cos^2(\tau X)}\\
 &\ge 1+0.08y^2\qquad(0\le y\le4).
\end{aligned}$$ For this last bound, the numerator is at least $1+y^2/X^2$, whereas $\cos(\tau X)>0.917$ and $\sinh(\tau y)\le1.082\tau y$ bound the denominator by $1+Ay^2$ with $A=(1.082\cdot0.172/0.917)^2$. The inequality follows from $X^{-2}\ge A+0.08+16(0.08)A$. Since $r+1\ge5$, <a href="#sig:ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:ratio">[sig:ratio]</a> now implies $$\begin{aligned}
 E_v
 &<\frac{1.435}{3.14}(0.955)^5
      \int_0^4(1+0.08y^2)^{-5/2}\,dy\\
 &<0.783.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the integral equals $$\frac{4(1+2\cdot0.08\cdot16/3)}{(1+0.08\cdot16)^{3/2}}
 <2.154.$$

On a horizontal side the identity for the modulus of tangent gives $$|Q(x+4i)|\ge\tanh4\,\tanh(4\tau),\qquad
 |\tan(b(x+4i))|\le\coth(4b)<1.001.$$ Since $\tau/\tanh(4\tau)$ is increasing, $$\frac{Q(\rho)}{\tanh4\,\tanh(4\tau)}
 <\frac{2.30\cdot0.172}{0.999\cdot0.595}.$$ Substitution in <a href="#sig:horizontal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:horizontal">[sig:horizontal]</a>, with $X/\pi=3/4$, therefore gives $$E_h<\frac{3\cdot1.435}{4}\,1.001
       \left(\frac{2.30\cdot0.172}{0.999\cdot0.595}\right)^5
 <0.142.$$ The numerical trigonometric inequalities used here can be checked without numerical approximation: use $$\sin t\le t-t^3/6+t^5/120,\qquad
 \cos t\ge1-t^2/2+t^4/24-t^6/720
 \quad(0\le t\le1.111)$$ for $\tan\rho$ and the smaller real arguments. For the hyperbolic inequalities use $$\frac{\sinh t}{t}\le1+\frac{t^2}{6}
       +\frac{t^4/120}{1-t^2/42}\quad(0\le t\le0.688).$$ The first seven terms of the exponential series at $1.376$ give $\tanh(0.688)>0.595$, while $e^8>2000$ gives $\tanh4>0.999$. The same bound and $b>\sqrt2$ imply $\coth(4b)<1.001$.

Finally $Q(\rho)<2.30\tau<1$, so <a href="#sig:main-term" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sig:main-term">[sig:main-term]</a> and $r+1\ge5$ imply $$L>\frac{\tau}{1+\tau^2}(2.30\tau)^{-5}
 \ge\frac{1}{(1+0.172^2)\,2.30^5\,0.172^4}>17.$$ Combining these estimates yields $$(-1)^r\sigma_r>(1-0.783-0.142)17=1.275>1,$$ contradicting either possible cohomological signature. Thus $s=1$. Restricting <a href="#inc:normal-classes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:normal-classes">[inc:normal-classes]</a> to a fixed-$p$ fiber gives $e(\mathcal V)=-u_2$ and $p_1(\mathcal V)=2u_2$ there. Proposition <a href="#inc:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:normal">14</a> identifies this restriction with the actual tautological four-plane bundle, which proves the last assertion as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### The geodesic space and its integral classes

<a id="geo:incidence-input"></a>
Throughout this section, $M$ has quaternionic model type, so that $$n=4r,\qquad r\ge2,\qquad d=4r-1,\qquad k=3,
 \qquad H^*(M;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z[u]/(u^{r+1}),\quad |u|=4.$$ We retain the normalization $D=\pi/2$ and $T=2D=\pi$. Choose the sign of $u$ as permitted by Theorem <a href="#sig:coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sig:coefficient">16</a>, so that the actual four-plane bundle $\mathcal V$ on cut incidence $F$ satisfies $$\label{geo:incidence-input}
 p_1(\mathcal V)=2(u_1+u_2).$$ Orient $M$ by $\int_Mu^r=1$. Only the characteristic-class conclusion <a href="#geo:incidence-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:incidence-input">[geo:incidence-input]</a> and the bundle identification in Proposition <a href="#inc:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:normal">14</a> are used from the incidence argument.

Let $U=SM$, let $\pi:U\to M$ be the unit tangent projection, and write $z=\pi^*u$. The prime period is $T$, so the action $$(\mathbb R/\mathbb Z)\times U\longrightarrow U,
 \qquad (a,w)\longmapsto\phi_{Ta}(w)$$ is free. Its quotient and quotient map will be denoted by $$B=U/S^1,\qquad \rho:U\longrightarrow B.$$ Thus $B$ is the closed smooth manifold of oriented, unmarked geodesics, of dimension $2n-2$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The contact normalization and the two Gysin sequences

The contact calculation is the volume method for periodic geodesic flows associated with Weinstein (Weinstein 1974) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Weinstein1974](#ref-Weinstein1974)]; we include it to fix the period, Euler sign and angular-area factors exactly. The ensuing integral Gysin sequences, universal coefficient theorem and Poincaré duality are used in their standard forms (Hatcher 2002; J. W. Milnor and Stasheff 1974) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002), [MilnorStasheff1974](#ref-MilnorStasheff1974)]. Yang’s complex-projective volume calculation also uses the Gysin sequences of the unit-tangent sphere bundle and the geodesic-circle bundle (Yang 1991, 381–82) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Yang1991](#ref-Yang1991)]. Here the degree-four generator leads to a different middle-degree lattice.

Let $\lambda$ be the unit tangent contact form, $$\lambda_w(\zeta)=\langle w,d\pi_w\zeta\rangle.$$ Its Reeb flow is the unit-speed geodesic flow. Consequently $\theta=\lambda/T$ is a connection for the above $\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ action: it is invariant and integrates to one on each orbit. Its curvature is the pullback of a symplectic form $\omega_B$ on $B$. We fix the Euler-class sign convention by $$\rho^*\omega_B=d\theta,\qquad e=[\omega_B]\in H^2(B;\mathbb Z),$$ and give $B$ its symplectic orientation.

<a id="stmt-geo-contact"></a>
<div id="geo:contact" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:contact-formula"></a>
<a id="geo:model-number"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Contact volume). *With this orientation and Euler class, $$\label{geo:contact-formula}
 N:=\int_B e^{4r-1}
   =\frac{(4r-1)!\,|S^{4r-1}|\,\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M)}{T^{4r}}>0.$$ For the standard quaternionic model at the same diameter, this number is $$\label{geo:model-number}
 N_0=\frac{1}{2r+1}\binom{4r}{2r}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-contact"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-contact</summary>

*Proof.* Integration over the circle fibers gives $$\int_Be^d
 =\int_U\theta\wedge(d\theta)^d
 =T^{-(d+1)}\int_U\lambda\wedge(d\lambda)^d,$$ where $U$ has the orientation induced by the positive circle direction and the chosen orientation of $B$. To compute the last form as a density, use the connection splitting of $T_wU$ into horizontal and vertical directions. Choose an orthonormal frame $(w,e_1,\ldots,e_d)$ at $\pi(w)$. The contact form reads off the horizontal $w$ coordinate, and $d\lambda$ pairs each horizontal $e_i$ coordinate with its vertical $e_i$ coordinate, up to one common sign convention. In the $d$-fold exterior power each pairing occurs once in each of $d!$ orders. Hence $$\big|\lambda\wedge(d\lambda)^d\big|=d!\,d\mu,$$ where $d\mu$ is base Riemannian volume times unit tangent sphere area. The chosen orientation makes the contact integral positive, and $\mu(U)=|S^d|\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(M)$. Since $d+1=4r$, this proves <a href="#geo:contact-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:contact-formula">[geo:contact-formula]</a>.

The model’s polar density on $(0,D)$ is $\sin^{4r-1}t\cos^3t$. With $y=\sin^2t$, $$\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{4r-1}t\cos^3t\,dt
 =\frac12\int_0^1 y^{2r-1}(1-y)\,dy
 =\frac{1}{4r(2r+1)}.$$ Using $|S^{4r-1}|=2\pi^{2r}/(2r-1)!$ and $T=\pi$ in <a href="#geo:contact-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:contact-formula">[geo:contact-formula]</a> gives $$N_0=\frac{(4r-1)!}{r(2r+1)((2r-1)!)^2}
     =\frac{(4r)!}{(2r)!(2r+1)!},$$ which is <a href="#geo:model-number" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:model-number">[geo:model-number]</a>. ◻

</details>

Thus the desired upper volume bound is $N\le N_0$. The integral cohomology calculation below computes the determinant of a middle pairing whose entries are top-degree products, including this characteristic number.

<a id="stmt-geo-gysin"></a>
<div id="geo:gysin" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:low-basis"></a>
<a id="geo:total-betti"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Integral Gysin information). *The manifolds $U$ and $B$ are simply connected. There is an integral class $v\in H^4(B;\mathbb Z)$ with $\rho^*v=z$. For $2j<n$, the following is an integral basis: $$\label{geo:low-basis}
 H^{2j}(B;\mathbb Z):\qquad
 e^{j-2a}v^a,\qquad 0\le a\le\lfloor j/2\rfloor.$$ All odd cohomology of $B$ vanishes, and its even cohomology is free abelian. In particular $H^2(B;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Ze$ and $H^4(B;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Ze^2\oplus\mathbb Zv$. The rational cohomology ring is generated by $e,v$, and $$\label{geo:total-betti}
 \dim_{\mathbb Q}H^*(B;\mathbb Q)=2r(r+1).$$*

<a id="geo:middle-sequence"></a>
<a id="geo:middle-form"></a>
*Multiplication by $e$ at the middle fits into an exact sequence $$\label{geo:middle-sequence}
 0\longrightarrow H^{n-2}(B;\mathbb Z)
 \xrightarrow{\ e\smile\ }H^n(B;\mathbb Z)
 \longrightarrow\mathbb Z/(r+1)\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently the integral bilinear form $$\label{geo:middle-form}
 (a,b)\longmapsto\int_B eab,\qquad a,b\in H^{n-2}(B;\mathbb Z),$$ has determinant of absolute value $r+1$ in any integral basis. The map $e\smile:H^n(B;\mathbb Z)\to H^{n+2}(B;\mathbb Z)$ is an isomorphism.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-gysin"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-gysin</summary>

*Proof.* The sphere fiber of $\pi$ has dimension $n-1\ge7$. Its homotopy sequence and simple connectivity of $M$ give $\pi_1(U)=0$. The homotopy sequence of the circle bundle then gives $\pi_1(B)=0$.

<a id="geo:unit-cohomology"></a>
The Euler number of $TM$ is $\chi(M)=r+1$. The chosen orientation therefore gives $$e(TM)=(r+1)u^r.$$ The integral Gysin sequence of the oriented sphere bundle $\pi$ has the form $$H^{q-n}(M)\xrightarrow{\ e(TM)\smile\ }H^q(M)
 \xrightarrow{\ \pi^*\ }H^q(U)
 \xrightarrow{\ \pi_!\ }H^{q-n+1}(M)
 \xrightarrow{\ e(TM)\smile\ }H^{q+1}(M).$$ It gives the complete additive calculation $$\label{geo:unit-cohomology}
 H^q(U;\mathbb Z)=
 \begin{cases}
  \mathbb Z\{z^a\},&q=4a,\quad 0\le a<r,\\
  \mathbb Z/(r+1)\{z^r\},&q=n,\\
  \mathbb Z\{y_a\},&q=n-1+4a,\quad 1\le a\le r,\\
  0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Here the upper generators may be chosen with $\pi_!y_a=u^a$ and $y_a=z^{a-1}y_1$; the latter assertion follows from the projection formula and the fact that $\pi_!$ is an isomorphism in these upper degrees. At degree $n-1$, the Gysin map from $H^0(M)$ to $H^n(M)$ is multiplication by the nonzero integer $r+1$, so $H^{n-1}(U)=0$. This also explains the sole torsion group in <a href="#geo:unit-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:unit-cohomology">[geo:unit-cohomology]</a>.

<a id="geo:low-exact"></a>
Apply next the integral Gysin sequence of $\rho$. The vanishing of odd groups of $U$ below degree $n$ inductively gives $H^{2j-1}(B)=0$ for $2j-1<n$. For positive even $2j<n$ the remaining terms give $$\label{geo:low-exact}
 0\longrightarrow H^{2j-2}(B)
 \xrightarrow{\ e\smile\ }H^{2j}(B)
 \xrightarrow{\ \rho^*\ }H^{2j}(U)
 \longrightarrow0.$$ For $2j=2$ this says $H^2(B)=\mathbb Ze$. For $2j=4$, the last group is freely generated by $z$, so choose an integral lift $v$. If $j$ is odd, the last group in <a href="#geo:low-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:low-exact">[geo:low-exact]</a> vanishes and multiplication by $e$ gives the next basis. If $j=2a$ is even, the last group is generated by $z^a$, and the class $v^a$ lifts that generator. Induction proves exactly the integral basis <a href="#geo:low-basis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:low-basis">[geo:low-basis]</a>.

In all degrees at most $n-2$, the homology groups of $B$ are free and have the same ranks as its cohomology groups. Indeed the universal coefficient theorem detects torsion in $H_i(B;\mathbb Z)$ as torsion in $H^{i+1}(B;\mathbb Z)$, and the latter groups are free through degree $n-1$. Poincaré duality on the oriented $(2n-2)$-manifold $B$ now supplies all its cohomology in degrees at least $n$. It is free in even degrees and zero in odd degrees; together with the low-degree calculation this proves the asserted global statement.

The circle Gysin sequence at degree $n$ uses $H^{n-1}(U)=H^{n-1}(B)=0$ and $H^n(U)=\mathbb Z/(r+1)$, and is precisely <a href="#geo:middle-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:middle-sequence">[geo:middle-sequence]</a>. The Poincaré pairing $$H^{n-2}(B;\mathbb Z)\times H^n(B;\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow\mathbb Z$$ is unimodular, since these two groups are free. Composing one factor with the injection of index $r+1$ in <a href="#geo:middle-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:middle-sequence">[geo:middle-sequence]</a> proves the determinant assertion <a href="#geo:middle-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:middle-form">[geo:middle-form]</a>. Finally $H^{n+1}(U)=H^{n+2}(U)=0$ by <a href="#geo:unit-cohomology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:unit-cohomology">[geo:unit-cohomology]</a>. The next portion of the circle Gysin sequence therefore makes multiplication by $e$ from degree $n$ to degree $n+2$ an isomorphism.

In every even degree $q\ge n$, the group $H^q(U;\mathbb Q)$ vanishes. The rational circle Gysin sequence therefore makes $e\smile:H^{q-2}(B;\mathbb Q)\to H^q(B;\mathbb Q)$ surjective. The low-degree bases now show that the full rational ring is generated by $e,v$. Below degree $n$, the basis ranks sum to $$\sum_{j=0}^{2r-1}\bigl(\lfloor j/2\rfloor+1\bigr)
 =2\sum_{a=0}^{r-1}(a+1)=r(r+1).$$ Poincaré duality supplies another $r(r+1)$ dimensions above degree $n-1$, and the middle odd group is zero. This proves <a href="#geo:total-betti" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:total-betti">[geo:total-betti]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The first free-loop attachment

The Gysin sequences determine the low-degree integral lattice and its middle determinant, but not the relations among $e$ and $v$. Those relations will come from the first nonconstant critical manifold of the free-loop energy.

Write $\Lambda M=H^1(\mathbb R/T\mathbb Z,M)$ for the Sobolev free loop space, with rotation of the parameter as its circle action. The continuous-loop and Sobolev-loop models have the same homotopy type, compatibly with evaluation and rotation. We use the energy $$\mathcal E(c)=\frac12\int_0^T|\dot c(t)|^2\,dt.$$ For a circle space $A$, write $A_{hS^1}=ES^1\times_{S^1}A$. The constant-loop subspace has trivial action, hence its Borel construction is $M\times BS^1$.

<a id="stmt-geo-attachment"></a>
<div id="geo:attachment" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (The first attachment and its coefficient). *There is an oriented rank-three vector bundle $E\to B$ and an attaching map $$f:S(E)\longrightarrow M.$$ Let $p:S(E)\to B$ and put $x=f^*u\in H^4(S(E);\mathbb Z)$. The following properties hold:*

1.  *The pullback $\rho^*E$ is the first negative bundle for the free-loop energy. It is isomorphic as a real vector bundle to the rank-three bundle $K\to U$ with fiber $$K_w=\{a\in w^\perp:
              J_a(D)=0\},
           \quad J_a(0)=0,\quad J_a'(0)=a,$$ where $J_a$ is the normal Jacobi field along $\gamma_w$.*

<a id="geo:attachment-pullback"></a>
2.  *Let $\widetilde p:S(\rho^*E)\to U$ and $\widehat\rho:S(\rho^*E)\to S(E)$ be the pullback square. Then $$\label{geo:attachment-pullback}
           \widehat\rho^*x=\widetilde p^*z.$$*

<a id="geo:primitive-boundary"></a>
3.  *After choosing the orientation of $E$, one has $$\label{geo:primitive-boundary}
           p_!x=e.$$ With either orientation its coefficient is $\pm1$.*

<a id="geo:negative-pontryagin"></a>
4.  *The actual negative bundle satisfies $$\label{geo:negative-pontryagin}
           \rho^*p_1(E)=4z.$$*

</div>

The attaching map makes $x^{r+1}=f^*(u^{r+1})=0$. The sphere integral $p_!x=e$ fixes the coefficient of the fiber part of $x$, while $\rho^*p_1(E)=4z$ carries the incidence coefficient to the actual negative bundle. Separating $x$ into base and fiber parts will turn its nilpotence into relations on $B$. The middle determinant will then fix the scale of the top-degree pairing used to evaluate $N$.

Figure <a href="#fig:attachment-pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:attachment-pullback">2</a> distinguishes the pullback square from the evaluation homotopy used in part <span class="upright">(ii)</span>.

<figure id="fig:attachment-pullback">

<figcaption>The sphere bundle of the descended rank-three bundle $E$ and its pullback to $U = SM$. The square is a pullback, with vertical fibers $S^{2}$ and horizontal fibers $S^{1}$. The attaching map $f$ is not point evaluation of an unmarked geodesic. Evaluation of the attachment gives the displayed homotopy and hence the equality of cohomology classes.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-attachment-pullback"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:attachment-pullback`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 28 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

